Annual Report (NBM, 2022)
Note
The Annual Report 2022 was examined and adopted by the
Supervisory Board of the National Bank of Moldova at meeting of
May 29, 2023 and was submitted to the Parliament of the
Republic of Moldova in accordance with the provisions of Law No.
548/1995 on the National Bank of Moldova (republished in
Monitorul Oﬁcial of the Republic of Moldova, 2015, No. 297-300,
art. 544, with subsequent amendments).
The Report was compiled using the latest statistical data held by
the National Bank of Moldova, the National Bureau of Statistics,
the Ministry of Economic Development and Digitalization and the
Ministry of Finance.
Also, were used data provided by international organizations and
central banks of neighboring countries.
Computation of some statisitcal data was conducted by the
National Bank of Moldova.
All rights reserved. No part of this publication may be reproduced,
and the use of data in studies is allowed with the proper
speciﬁcation of the source.
The Annual Report 2022 is a translation of Raport anual 2022 in
Romanian. In case of discrepancies, the version of the Report in
the original language prevails.

Annual Report (NBM, 2022)
Foreword
The year 2022 was marked and overshadowed by the war in
Ukraine, and on the economic front – by its consequences, which,
superimposed with multiple external and internal shocks, made
this year an extremely difﬁcult one in economic and monetary
terms. Also, the prompt and decisive monetary policy measures
taken by the National Bank enabled us to overcome the inﬂationary
wave that Moldova faced and, towards the end of the year, to move
ﬁrmly onto a downward trend in inﬂation.
At the beginning of 2022, the economy of the Republic of Moldova
was impacted by several events and shocks, the effects of which
were still diffusing.
The economy was recovering from the
pandemic crisis, food prices on the international market were at
record highs, energy prices were at historic highs, and rising
social payments were fuelling pro-inﬂationary tendencies. In 2022,
the
series
of
inﬂation-fuelled
shocks
continued
with
tariff
adjustments and culminated in the outbreak of war in Ukraine,
which disrupted supply chains and led to Ukrainian products being
replaced by more expensive ones.
The monetary policy tightening, which started in the summer of
2021 in anticipation of the inﬂationary wave, continued through
most of 2022 and was commensurate and appropriate to price
forecasts.
This allowed us to halt and reverse the trend of
accelerating price growth, and at the end of the year to start the
monetary policy easing cycle. In the absence of further external
and internal shocks, monetary policy easing will continue, with a
view to bringing the inﬂation rate back into the corridor and
creating the right conditions for the country’s economy to recover.
The reform of the banking system over the past few years has
ensured a high level of resilience in the banking sector, which
has allowed it to get through this difﬁcult year without being
signiﬁcantly affected. The stability of the banking system was all


Foreword
5
the more important in the initial phase of the war, when banks
were coping with a considerable volume of deposit withdrawals
and servicing payments for the inﬂux of refugees from Ukraine.
The banking system remains available to ﬁnance growth at the
start of the next economic cycle.
The responsible lending regulations implemented during 2022 in
the banking and non-banking sector are a major addition to the
ﬁnancial stability measurement toolkit, designed to eliminate
household over-indebtedness and ensure that further credit
expansion will be sustainable and robust.
The National Bank’s ofﬁcial reserve assets continued to grow
during the year, reaching USD 4.5 billion. Their volume provides
a sufﬁcient and comfortable level of cover for international trade
operations and protection against possible shocks to ensure the
resilience of the national currency and its stability.
2023 will be a year of recovery. Easing monetary conditions will
create the prerequisites for the economy to recover.
Lower
inﬂation will bring the necessary predictability and stability to
economic activity, and the banking system is prepared to provide
ﬁnancing for companies and the population. In 2023, important
parts of the non-bank ﬁnancial system – insurance companies,
non-bank lending organisations, etc.
– will come under the
oversight of the National Bank, thus ensuring homogeneous and
consistent regulation and supervision across the spectrum of
ﬁnancial activities.
Octavian Armas, u
Governor


6
Annual Report (NBM, 2022)
Summary
International context
The year 2022 was rather challenging for the global economy,
with the post-pandemic economic recovery compromised by the
unprecedented rise in commodity prices against a backdrop of
signiﬁcant risks and uncertainties. On 24 February 2022, the war
between the Russian Federation and Ukraine, both major exporters
of raw materials, began, disrupting the traditional Black Sea trade
routes. At the same time, the economic sanctions imposed on the
Russian Federation and the halt in natural gas exports to Europe
have caused a major energy crisis on the European continent,
with natural gas prices rising by a factor of tens compared to
historical averages. Rising production costs and the recovery in
global demand quickly contributed to rising consumer prices and,
in response, the authorities tightened monetary conditions. As a
result, monetary policy interest rates rose at the end of 2022 to
4.5% in the US, 3.5% in the UK and 2.5% in the euro area. In the
region, the economic challenges were even more severe because
of the proximity to the conﬂict zone and the huge dependence on
energy resources from the Russian Federation, with inﬂation above
20% in the Baltic countries, Ukraine and the Republic of Moldova.
Annual inﬂation of 85.6% was recorded in October 2022 in Türkiye
against the background of the considerable depreciation of the
Türkiye lira and the unconventional policy of lowering interest
rates by the Central Bank of Türkiye. In 2022, food prices on the
international market increased by an average of 14.3% compared
to 2021 as a result of higher costs for energy resources, fertilizers,
transport and logistics costs.
Developments of economic activity in
the Republic of Moldova
In 2022, economic activity in the Republic of Moldova has seen
a sharp decline after the positive dynamics of 2021. As a result,
GDP decreased by 5.9% compared to the level in 2021. Among the
factors that led to a slowdown in economic activity during 2022
are the decline in the real incomes of the population in the context
of accelerating inﬂation, rising interest rates that slowed lending,
increased uncertainty in the region as a result of the escalation of
the armed conﬂict in Ukraine, the real appreciation of the national
currency against the currencies of major trading partners and, as
a result, a decline in the competitiveness of domestic products.
At the same time, in the second part of the year the contraction
in GDP was also driven by a lower performance of the agricultural
sector as a result of the dry conditions in the summer of 2022.
In terms of uses, household consumption decreased by 4.0%
compared to 2021.


Summary
7
On the other hand,
general government ﬁnal consumption
increased by 4.1%. Gross capital formation during the period had
a negative impact of 1.9 percentage points on GDP dynamics.
Exports, in real terms, increased by 24.9%.
A pronounced
negative impact on GDP dynamics in 2022 was caused by a 15.8%
increase in imports. Within the sectors of the national economy,
the agricultural sector contracted by 25.8%. At the same time, a
signiﬁcant negative impact was caused by the decrease in added
value in the industry.
The construction sector continued the
decline seen in 2021, contracting by 10.0% compared to the
previous year, and added value in the real estate transactions
sector contracted by 10.9%. A downward trend was also recorded
in transport.
Net taxes on products generated a signiﬁcant
negative
contribution
to
GDP
dynamics.
However,
the
above-mentioned negative contributions were partly mitigated by
developments in ﬁnancial and insurance activities, information
and communication, accommodation and food service activities,
and trade.
Inﬂation developments
In 2022, the average annual CPI rate was 28.7%, 23.6 percentage
points higher than in 2021. During 2022, the annual inﬂation rate
continued the pronounced upward trajectory of the previous year
until October 2022. Thus, it increased from 13.9% in December
2021 to 34.6% in October 2022. Subsequently, in the last two
months of the year, it showed a downward trend, reaching 30.2%
in December 2022.
The acceleration of prices in the ﬁrst ten months of the year was
supported by the increase in international and regional prices for
food and energy resources, which led to higher prices on the
domestic market, as well as the adjustment of several tariffs in
regulated prices. At the same time, the adjustment of tariffs, the
increase in fuel prices and the upward trend in wages supported
the increase in costs for economic agents which was gradually
reﬂected in prices.
The impact of the above factors was
signiﬁcantly ampliﬁed in the ﬁrst half of 2022 by the escalation of
the situation in Ukraine which generated additional pressures on
food, fuel and some core inﬂation subcomponents.
Additional
pressures on domestic food prices in the second part of 2022 were
caused by the hydrological drought of the previous summer. At
the
same
time,
for
some
CPI
subcomponents,
additional
inﬂationary pressures were driven by the dynamics of the
MDL/USD exchange rate in the ﬁrst part of 2022. According to the
estimates, the demand-side pressures observed during 2021 lost
their intensity in the ﬁrst part of 2022 and subsequently eased the
inﬂationary pressures generated by the above-mentioned factors.
Towards the end of 2022, the moderation in the annual CPI rate
was supported by the effect of a high base period at the end of
2021, subdued demand from the population in the context of
falling disposable income of the population in real terms, and
compensation to the population for energy resources in the cold
period of the year.


8
Annual Report (NBM, 2022)
Promoting monetary policy
The National Bank of Moldova promoted a tightening monetary
policy in 2022, to moderate the alert growth rates of consumer
prices, mitigating the side effects of supply shocks, stimulating
ﬁnancial intermediation in the national currency and saving at
the expense of consumption, balancing the trade balance, and
anchoring inﬂationary expectations and anticipations.
At the same time, the magnitude of monetary policy measures was
calibrated towards the end of the year with monetary policy easing
measures, in the context of the prospect of a reversal of the annual
inﬂation trajectory after the October 2022 peak and the emergence
of pronounced disinﬂationary pressures from aggregate demand
amid a negative GDP deviation.
In this regard, the members of the Executive Board of the NBM
decided to increase the monetary policy interest rate from 6.50%
in December 2021 to 21.50% annually in December 2022. The
aim of raising the base rate was to create the monetary conditions
necessary to temper the growth rates of consumer prices. At the
same time, the required reserve ratio of funds attracted in MDL
increased during 2022 from 26.0% to 40.0%.
At the meeting of the Executive Board of the NBM held on 5
December 2022, the decision to resume the monetary policy
easing measures, to lower the base rate by 1.5 percentage points
to 20.0%, was approved, which was conditional on the conﬁrmation
of the veracity of the forecast of the National Bank of Moldova and
its associated disinﬂationary trend. At the same time, the decision
to decrease the reserve requirement ratio of means attracted in
MDL and non-convertible foreign exchange assets from 40.0%
to 34.0%, by 3.0 percentage points in December 2022 and by
3.0 percentage points in January 2023, led to additional liquidity
inﬂows into the money market, contributing to an increase in credit
resources for the real and public sectors.
The annual growth rate of the broad money supply in 2022 was
5.3%. The M2 monetary aggregate grew by 9.6% annually, and
money in circulation increased by 8.5% annually. The average
interest rate on new loans granted in national currency in 2022
was 11.73% per annum, while the average rate on new attracted
deposits was 10.61% per annum.
Banking sector
As of 31 December 2022, 11 banks licensed by the National Bank
of Moldova (NBM) were operating in the Republic of Moldova.
The banking sector in 2022, according to the data submitted by
banks, was characterized by high liquidity, growth of assets, loans,
own funds, deposits of individuals, deposits of legal entities and
deposits of banks. The proﬁt for the year compared to the previous
year also increased, mainly as a result of higher interest and non-
interest income. The highest interest income is earned on loan


Summary
9
investments, followed by income from funds placed with the NBM
(required reserves) and income from debt securities (SS, NBC).
During this period, there was an increase in the absolute value
of overdue loans, non-performing loans and, as a result, a slight
worsening of loan portfolio quality indicators compared to the end
of the previous year.
It should be noted that, at the same time, there was also an
increase in allowances for formed impairment losses (IFRS
allowances), leading to an increase in the share of allowances for
formed impairment losses on loans (IFRS allowances) in calculated
allowances on loans (prudential allowances) to 95.3%.
In 2022, the banking sector continued the trend of consolidation
of own funds, which amounted to MDL 18.4 billion, increasing by
21.2%.
At the same time, all banks complied with the "Total own funds
ratio" indicator, including capital buffers. The total own funds ratio
for the banking sector according to the data submitted by licensed
banks was 29.5%, an increase of 3.6 percentage points compared
to the end of the previous year.
Banks maintained a high level of liquidity and proﬁtability during
the reporting period.
Thus, the value of the long-term liquidity indicator (liquidity
principle I) was 0.67 (limit ≤1), which is insigniﬁcantly lower by
0.1 percentage points compared to the end of the previous year.
In addition, as of 31 December 2022, all banks also complied with
liquidity principle III, which is the ratio of adjusted effective liquidity
to required liquidity in each maturity band (limit ≥1).
The liquidity coverage ratio by sector was 267.9%, decreasing by
90.4 percentage points compared to the end of the previous year.
This indicator was met by all banks (limit ≥80% – from 1 January
2022 to 1 January 2023).
The proﬁt for the ﬁnancial year ended on 31 December 2022 in the
banking sector amounted to MDL 3.6 billion, increasing by 58.6%
compared to the previous year. For the year 2022, the return on
assets2 and capital3 of licensed banks recorded values of 2.9%
and 16.9% respectively, increasing by 0.9 percentage points and
4.6 percentage points compared to the end of the previous year.
Assets and deposits also continued to grow by 10.9% to MDL 131.4
billion and by 5.4% to MDL 95.0 billion, respectively.
Compared to the end of the previous year, the loan portfolio in
the banking sector increased by 9.3% to MDL 61.6 billion. During
the year, the share of non-performing loans in the loan portfolio
increased insigniﬁcantly by 0.3 percentage points to 6.4%.
The National Bank carries out ex ofﬁcio supervision based on the
annual prudential supervision programme.
For 2022,
the
prudential supervision programme included various ongoing
2Return on assets = annualized net income/ average assets
3Return on capital = annualized net income/ average share capital


10
Annual Report (NBM, 2022)
actions (monitoring of key indicators, analysis of ﬁnancial reports
submitted by banks,
external audit reports,
monitoring of
remediation plans following on-site inspections) as well as actions
for
the
preparation
of
the
SREP
report
(business
model
assessment, governance and internal control analysis, capital and
liquidity risk assessment, analysis of ICAAP reports, etc.).
Thus, the results of the supervision and evaluation process (SREP)
for 2020 were prepared and sent to all 11 banks. The results of the
supervision and business review process (SREP) for 2021 have also
been prepared and sent to 6 banks. All banks also complied with
the additional own funds requirement set by the NBM following
the results of the supervision and evaluation of banks’ activities
(SREP).
During 2022, 4 thematic inspections were carried out at licensed
banks, including 3 planned inspections and 1 unannounced
inspection.
Also, during the reporting year, decisions of the Executive Board of
the NBM related to thematic controls on the activity of 6 licensed
banks were approved.
As a result, the following were applied:
- warnings to 3 banks, 8 members of the Bank’s Board, 3 members
of the Bank’s Executive Body and 1 Head of the Internal Audit
Section,
- ﬁnes to 2 banks, 1 member of the Bank’s Board, 5 members of the
Bank’s Executive Body, and 2 heads of internal control functions.


11
Chapter 1
International context
1.1
Global
economy,
ﬁnancial
and
commodity markets
The year 2022 proved to be relatively complicated for the global
economy.
The
post-pandemic
economic
recovery
was
compromised by the unprecedented rise in commodity prices,
amid signiﬁcant risks and uncertainties. On 24 February 2022 the
war between the Russian Federation and Ukraine, both major
exporters of raw materials, began, disrupting the traditional Black
Sea trade routes. Therewith, the economic sanctions imposed on
the Russian Federation and the halt of natural gas exports to
Europe have caused a major energy crisis on the European
continent, and natural gas prices have risen tens of times higher
compared to historical averages.
Rising production costs and the recovery in global demand
contributed to a rapid rise in consumer prices and, in response,
the authorities tightened monetary conditions. Thereby, monetary
policy interest rates increased at the end of 2022 to 4.5% in the
US, 3.5% in the UK and 2.5% in the Euro area.
In the region, the economic challenges in 2022 were even greater
due
to
the
proximity
of
the
conﬂict
zone
and
the
huge
dependence on energy resources from the Russian Federation,
with inﬂation of over 20% in the Baltic countries, Ukraine and the
Republic of Moldova. An annual inﬂation of 85.6% was recorded in
October 2022 in Türkiye against the backdrop of the signiﬁcant
depreciation of the Türkiye lira and the unconventional policy of
lowering interest rates by the Central Bank of Türkiye. In 2022,
food prices on the international market increased by an average
of 14.3% compared to 2021 due to higher costs for energy
resources, fertilizers, transport and logistics costs.

In 2022, economic growth in most countries was lower than in
2021,
largely as a result of the faster recovery from the
consequences of the pandemic crisis and new challenges, such as
rising prices that led to an immediate and substantial tightening
of monetary conditions. The United States of America recorded
economic growth of 2.1% in 2022.
In the UK, gross domestic
product growth was 4.1%, despite accelerated interest rate hikes
by the Bank of England and ﬁscal and ﬁnancial problems that
caused the government to change twice during 2022. In the Euro
area, economic growth was 3.5%, of which Spain – 5.5%, Italy –
3.7%, France – 2.6% and Germany – 1.8%. The challenges of the
energy crisis have signiﬁcantly reduced the growth potential of
Euro area economies, affecting in particular countries with a high
degree of dependence on pipeline gas imports from the Russian


12
Annual Report (NBM, 2022)
Federation. Eastern EU countries such as Poland and Romania
recorded robust increases in gross domestic product of 4.9 and
4.7% respectively, beneﬁting from increased consumption related
to a large number of refugees from Ukraine, but also from low
energy dependency. The economy of Türkiye grew by an average
of 5.6% in 2022, beneﬁting along with Poland and Romania from
its increased importance in regional trade following the war in
Ukraine. The economy of the Russian Federation shrank by only
2.1% in 2022, much less than forecasts in the spring of 2022
when
extremely
tough
economic
sanctions
were
imposed
designed to limit revenues, and therefore the ability to fund the
war. However, the surge in oil, gas, fertiliser and other commodity
prices to record highs has helped boost Russian export earnings.
Between January and December 2022, the economy of Ukraine
shrank by an average of 30.1%, as the war crippled economic
activity, limited transport across the Black Sea and, worst of all,
irreparably damaged its energy infrastructure. For comparison, in
2022, the economy of Moldova shrank by 5.9%, factors discussed
in Chapter 2 of this report (Chart 1.1).
From January to October 2022, the US dollar has appreciated at
an unprecedented rate due to increased global risks and tighter
monetary conditions by the Federal Reserve System (FRS) than
in other major economies. Global risks were associated ﬁrst with
the war in Ukraine and then with the signiﬁcant rise in commodity
prices. Also, since March, the FRS has gradually increased the

interest rate range to 4.25-4.5% in December 2022 (Chart 1.2).
In June 2022, annual inﬂation in the US reached a 40-year high
of 9.1%, subsequently falling to 6.5% in December 2022, but still
outside the FRS’s 2.0% medium-term target.
In 2022, the European single currency depreciated, reaching in
September 2022 slightly below parity against the US dollar (around
0.96 EUR/USD). The underlying reasons for the depreciation of the
EUR were the impending consequences of the war in Ukraine on
European economies and the resulting worsening of the energy
crisis. Although the volume of natural gas transported by pipeline
from Russia to Europe started to decline in the second half of 2021,
the risks worsened with the outbreak of the war in Ukraine and the
mutual imposition of economic sanctions between the economies
concerned. Concerns about a shortage of natural gas for the cold
Chart 1.3: Evolution EUR/USD and 3 months Euribor
Source: ECB
season in Europe have gradually increased and high energy prices
have led to a decline in production activity. However, towards
the end of 2022, high temperatures, sufﬁcient natural gas stocks
and energy-saving measures increased conﬁdence that the energy
crisis in Europe would be overcome at least for the 2022-2023 cold
season, leading to an appreciation of the European single currency.
Another important factor for the evolution of the European single
currency in 2022 was the ECB’s monetary policy. In the ﬁrst part of
2022 when most monetary authorities were raising interest rates,
the ECB kept rates at zero, even though inﬂation was rising at a
fast pace, which ampliﬁed the depreciation of the European single
currency.


Chapter 1. International context
13
Starting in July 2022, the ECB began steadily raising interest rates
to a level of 2.5% in December 2022, which convinced ﬁnancial
markets of the ECB’s determination to stabilize inﬂation, which
ampliﬁed the appreciation of the European single currency in the
last months of 2022 (Chart 1.3).

In 2022, most currencies followed a depreciation path against
the US dollar amid rising risks from pro-inﬂationary pressures,
geopolitical and supply risks, and international trade restructuring
(Table 1.1). However, it should be noted that the Türkiye lira has
depreciated on average by 86.4% as a result of the unconventional
monetary policy of the Central Bank of Türkiye. Although annual
inﬂation gradually increased to a peak of 85.6% in October 2022,
the Central Bank of Türkiye already in August 2022 gradually
lowered the monetary policy rate from 14.0 to 9.0% in November
2022. At the same time, the Türkiye authorities have introduced
strict domestic controls on foreign exchange transactions and
foreign currency deposits, which has further decreased foreign
investor conﬁdence.
Another currency that performed differently in 2022 was the
Russian ruble, which depreciated to 177 RUB/USD in March
2022 and then appreciated to 50 RUB/USD in June 2022. The
Russian ruble depreciated rapidly after 24 February 2022 as
sanctions were phased in to isolate Russia economically and limit
the revenue from commodity exports. In response, the Bank of
Russia introduced a series of measures aimed at curbing ﬁnancial
capital
outﬂows
from
the
country
and
limiting
currency
speculation on the domestic market.
This led initially to the
stabilisation and subsequently to the appreciation of the Russian
ruble.
Also, high prices for energy resources and other raw
materials in 2022 led to a signiﬁcant increase in foreign exchange
earnings from exports, which contributed to the sustainability of
the national budget of the Russian Federation.
Among international currencies, the UK pound sterling stood out
in 2022, hitting a record low against the US dollar at the end of
September 2022 as investors worried about announcements of
tax cuts and massive aid to pay bills. This triggered a massive
sell-off in long-term UK government bonds, prompting the Bank of
England to step in and stabilise the market.

In 2022, international market prices continued to rise at a
rapid pace, amplifying the effects of the price increase in 2021.
However, while in 2021 the underlying factor behind the increase
in international market prices was the post-pandemic recovery
in economic activity, in 2022 prices increased largely as a result
of supply shocks, geopolitical risks, wage increases and second-
round effects. Thereby, according to price indices calculated by the
World Bank (WB), fertilizer prices have increased by an average
of 54.8%, after an equally signiﬁcant increase of 104.1% in 2021.
The price of Brent oil in 2022 saw an average increase of 41.7%
compared to 2021, with a peak of USD 139/barrel in early March
2022.
Food prices on the international market, as reﬂected by the
FAO index, rose by an average of 14.3% in 2022, largely due
to rising production costs (fertilizers and energy resources), but
also to the blockage of grain transport across the Black Sea.
At the same time, metal and mineral prices, according to
indices calculated by the World Bank, fell by an average of 1.2%


14
Annual Report (NBM, 2022)
in 2022 compared to 2021, due to the maintenance of COVID-
19 restrictions in China, which substantially reduced production
activity (Chart 1.4).
The economic sanctions imposed on the Russian Federation and
the halt of natural gas exports to Europe has caused an
unprecedented energy crisis on the European continent, and
natural gas prices have risen dozens of times above historical
averages. The Netherlands TTF price exceeded USD 3,800/1,000
cubic metres in early March 2022 due to increased geopolitical
risks. Another peak of over USD 3,500/1,000 cubic metres was
reached in August 2022 after the Nord Stream 1 pipeline was
damaged. Thus, 2022 can be characterised by countries’ rush for
new sources of LNG4, by increasing investment in alternative
energy
sources
and
new
LNG
marine
receiving
terminals.
However, in the last months of 2022, natural gas reserves in
Europe were sufﬁcient for the cold season and LNG supply was in
surplus, causing natural gas prices to fall, although not to
historical levels (Chart 1.5).

Source: US Energy Information Administration, NBM

The oil market recovered in 2022, with global supply increasing
from 95.7 million barrels per day in 2021 to 99.8 million barrels
per day in 2022. At the same time, oil demand recovered more
slowly, from 97.1 million barrels per day in 2021 to 99.4 million
barrels per day in 2022.
Although OPEC+ has continued to maintain its plans to limit oil
production, expectations of China’s economic recovery have not
materialised, which has to some extent created a surplus of oil
on the world market. Also, the Russian Federation in search of
new markets and ways to circumvent economic sanctions has
not strictly followed the production plan. Overall, the oil market
underwent signiﬁcant structural changes in 2022, with traditional
oil markets and transportation routes being radically changed
(Chart 1.6).
1.2
Evolution of important economies
United States of America
In 2022, US gross domestic product grew 2.1% after growing 5.9%
in 2021. The average US unemployment rate in 2022 was 3.6%
and the average inﬂation rate was 8.0%, peaking at 9.1% in June
2022. In 2022, industrial production in the US increased by 3.4%.
Euro area and the European Union
In 2022, the combined gross domestic product of the Euro area
and the European Union both grew by 3.5%, compared to 5.3 and
5.4% respectively in 2021. According to a ﬁrst estimate of annual
growth for 2022, based on quarterly data, employment increased
by 2.5% in the Euro area and 2.1% in the EU. The unemployment
rate in the Euro area averaged 6.7%, and 6.1% in the EU. Inﬂation
has risen signiﬁcantly, averaging 8.4% per year in the Euro area
and 9.2% in the EU. Industrial production increased by 2.2% in the
4Liqueﬁed natural gas


Chapter 1. International context
15
Euro area in 2022, as industrial activity was strongly affected by
the energy crisis. At the same time, construction output increased
by 2.5% in the reference period compared to the previous year.
External trade in the Euro area showed a signiﬁcant development,
with exports increasing by 18.0% and imports by 37.5% (Table
1.2).
China
The slowdown in China’s real GDP in the fourth quarter of 2022
has caused average annual economic growth to moderate to 3.0%,
the ﬁrst time in more than 40 years that growth has been below
the global average.

The economy of Romania recorded a 4.7% increase in gross
domestic product.
Construction output grew by 12.9%, while
industrial production contracted by an average of 1.7%. Foreign
trade developed, with exports increasing by 23.4% and imports by
28.4%. Consumer prices increased in line with regional trends and
recorded an average annual rate of 13.8% (Table 1.2).
Poland
The economy of Poland grew by 4.9% in gross domestic product,
taking over a signiﬁcant share of transport between Europe and
Ukraine,
but
also
beneﬁting
from
increased
consumption
generated by a large number of Ukrainian refugees. Industrial
production grew by 11.0% and the construction sector increased
by 8.2%. Exports advanced by an average of 22.1% and imports
rose by an average of 28.5%.
As in other Eastern European
economies, consumer prices rose signiﬁcantly, by an average of
14.4% (Table 1.2).
Ukraine
The economy of Ukraine has been severely damaged by the
ongoing war, with gross domestic product shrinking by 30.1%
in 2022. From the little data that has been published, it shows
exports shrinking by 35.1% and imports by 24.1%. At the same
time, for 2022 industrial production in Ukraine has decreased by
36.9% and construction production by 65.1%. Consumer prices
have risen considerably, registering an average annual rate of
20.2% (Table 1.2).
Türkiye
Türkiye was another economy that took over some of the regional
transport and consumer demand following the outbreak of war in
Ukraine. Gross domestic product in Türkiye grew by 5.6% in 2022,
with industrial production increasing by 5.7% and construction
output doubling compared to 2021. The depreciation of the Türkiye
lira contributed to a 12.9% increase in exports, while imports grew
by 34.0%. Consumer prices rose to a record high, with the average
annual rate of inﬂation registering 72.3% (Table 1.2).


16
Annual Report (NBM, 2022)
Russian Federation
In 2022, the Russian economy contracted by 2.1%, according to
ofﬁcial data, which is signiﬁcantly less than assumed in the spring
when the main economic sanctions were announced. Revenue
from exports of energy resources and other raw materials was
considerable and offset losses in other areas. Industrial production
contracted by 0.6% from a year earlier, exports increased by 19.9%
and imports contracted by 11.7%. Against the backdrop of falling
domestic demand and the unprecedented appreciation of the
Russian ruble, annual consumer price growth rates in the Russian
Federation began to moderate in the second half of 2022. Overall,
in 2022 consumer prices in the Russian Federation increased by
an average of 13.8%.


17
Chapter 2
Economy of the Republic of Moldova
2.1
Real sector
Gross domestic product
In 2022, economic activity in the Republic of Moldova recorded
a pronounced decline after the positive dynamics of 2021. As a
result, GDP decreased by 5.9% compared to the level in 2021. In
the ﬁrst half of the year economic activity stagnated, after which
in the third and fourth quarters of 2022, GDP contracted by 10.3%
and 10.6%, respectively, from the corresponding periods in 2021.
Among the factors that led to a slowdown in economic activity
during 2022 and especially in the second half of the year, it is worth
mentioning the decline in real incomes of the population in the
context of accelerating inﬂation, rising interest rates, which slowed
lending, increased uncertainty in the region due to the escalation of
the conﬂict in Ukraine, the appreciation in real terms of the national
currency against the currencies of major trading partners and,
as a consequence, a decline in the competitiveness of domestic
products. At the same time, the contraction of GDP in the second
half of the year was also driven by a weaker performance of the
agricultural sector as a result of dry conditions in the summer of
the previous year.

In terms of uses (Chart 2.1), in 2022 household consumption
decreased by 4.0% compared to 2021. The fall in consumption
has been supported by the decline in real disposable income of
the population against the background of accelerating inﬂation
and tighter credit conditions. On the other hand, in 2022 ﬁnal
consumption of general government increased by 4.1%. Gross
capital formation in the reference period had a negative impact
of 1.9 percentage points on GDP dynamics. Exports, in real terms,
increased by 24.9% and had a positive impact of 7.6 percentage
points. A pronounced negative impact (-9.2 percentage points) on
GDP dynamics in 2022 was caused by a 15.8% increase in imports.
By resource category, the decline in GDP in 2022 was driven by
developments in most sectors of the economy.
However, the
most pronounced negative contributions were generated by the
dynamics of agriculture, industry and construction (Chart 2.2).
Thereby, the agricultural sector contracted by 25.8% and had
a negative impact of -2.7 percentage points on GDP growth in
the reference period. The pronounced decrease in agricultural
production was driven by dry weather conditions in the summer
of 2022, which reduced the average harvest per hectare for most
agricultural crops. At the same time, a signiﬁcant negative impact
(-1.2 percentage points) was caused by the decrease in value
added in industry. Within this, the energy industry contracted


18
Annual Report (NBM, 2022)
by 18.0%, manufacturing – by 7.5%, and mining and quarrying
decreased by 27.9% compared to 2021. The construction sector
continued the decline observed in 2021, contracting by 10.0%
compared to the previous year, and the value added related to the
real estate transactions sector contracted by 10.9%. Thus, these
sectors made negative contributions of -0.8 percentage points to
GDP dynamics, affected both by the signiﬁcant increase in prices
of construction materials, the tightening of credit conditions and
the decline in real disposable income of the population, as well
as the uncertainty related to the situation in the region. GVA in
transport and storage declined by 2.5% and had a negative impact
of 0.1 percentage points on GDP dynamics. At the same time, net
taxes on products decreased by 7.6%, generating a contribution
of -1.1 percentage points to GDP dynamics.

household ﬁnal consumption decreased by 4.0%
compared to the previous year (Chart 2.3). This was driven by a
5.9% decrease in expenditure on services and a 2.5% decrease in
expenditure on goods.

Negative developments in household consumption were supported
by a decline in disposable income and a decrease in the volume
of credit in the context of high interest rates during the period.
As a result, in 2022 the payroll fund increased by about 15.7%.
However, given that the average annual inﬂation rate in 2022
was 28.6%, the payroll fund in real terms is estimated to have
contracted by about 9.9% and to have had a pronounced negative
impact on potential sources of ﬁnancing for consumption. A similar
effect is observed for other sources of consumption ﬁnancing,
such as social insurances or remittances (Chart 2.4). At the same
time, the increase in lending rates led to a sharp contraction in
the volume of domestic currency lending to individuals, which
signiﬁcantly dampened domestic consumption.
Investments
In 2022, investment had a negative impact of 1.9 percentage
points on GDP growth, mainly due to the contribution of gross ﬁxed
capital formation (-1.7 percentage points). It recorded a decrease
of 6.8%. Within this, investment in machinery and equipment
fell by 0.4% and investment in construction contracted by 9.9%.
5Wholesale and retail trade; maintenance and repair of motor vehicles and

Changes in inventories had an impact of -0.2 percentage points.
Negative investment dynamics over the period were negatively

tighter credit conditions. In addition, the global increase in raw
material prices and transport costs have affected companies’
activity, disrupting investment plans.

During the reporting year, investments in ﬁxed assets contracted
by 11.6% compared to the previous year. Intangible ﬁxed assets
increased by 7.0%, while tangible ﬁxed assets contracted by 12.0%.
Within the latter, investment in residential, non-residential and
engineering buildings contracted by 13.3%, 13.7% and 21.4%
respectively. At the same time, investment in transport equipment
and machinery and equipment decreased by 7.1% and 4.6%
respectively (Chart 2.5).
From the perspective of the sources
of ﬁnancing of investments in ﬁxed assets, their dynamics was
mainly determined by the contracting of investments ﬁnanced
from own sources and related to the sub-component other sources.
At the same time, a negative impact, but of a lesser magnitude,
was determined by the decrease in investments ﬁnanced from the
budgets of the ATU, the state budget and the means of foreign
investors (Chart 2.6).

In 2022, compared to 2021, overall agricultural production
decreased in comparable prices by 29.8% (Chart 2.7).
This
dynamic was supported by negative developments in the crop
(-36.8%) and livestock (-2.6%) sectors.
Thus,
the largest
decreases in production volumes were recorded for corn for grain
(-74.8%), sugar beet (-49.6%), wheat (-45.6%), sunﬂower (-35.3%)
and fruit, nuts and berries (-26.1%).
At the same time, the
contraction in the volume of output in the livestock sector was
driven by the decline in milk and meat production.
It is worth noting that the livestock sector has not shown signs of
recovery in 2022 either. Thus, for the sixth consecutive year, the
volume of livestock production has contracted.

The volume of industrial production by all forms of ownership
decreased in real terms by 5.1% compared to 2021 (Chart 2.8).
This development was mainly due to a 4.5% decrease in
manufacturing industry (the sub-component with the largest
share).
At the same time, the "production and distribution of
electricity and heat, gas, hot water and air conditioning" and
mining and quarrying sectors recorded decreases of 8.4% and
4.9% respectively. In manufacturing industry, the most signiﬁcant
decreases were recorded in the following industrial activities:
"manufacture of electrical equipment" (-59.4%), "manufacture of
motor vehicles, trailers and semi-trailers" (-34.1%), "manufacture
of furniture" (-29.8%), "metallurgical industry" (-27.3%) and
"manufacture of machinery and equipment" (-24.4%).
On the
other hand, signiﬁcant positive developments in 2022 were
recorded in the food industry (12.1%), leather processing (14.1%),
wood processing (21.4%), pharmaceuticals (19.7%), computer,
electronic and optical manufacturing (14.9%).

Passenger transport
In 2022, rail, road, ﬂuvial, air and trolleybus companies carried
25.1% more passengers than in 2021 (Chart 2.10). This increase
was mainly due to a year-on-year increase in the number of
passengers transported by trolleybus (21.2%) and by road
(34.1%). At the same time, the number of passengers transported
by air increased by 43.2% and the number of passengers
transported by ﬂuvial and rail decreased year-on-year by 21.3 and
4.3% respectively.

In 2022, the annual rate of turnover for enterprises with the
main activity of retail trade decreased to -1.8% (in comparable
prices), which is 14.0 percentage points lower than in 2021. At the

services to households increased by 10.4% year-on-year over the

points lower than in the previous year.
Wholesale trade
In 2022, turnover in enterprises principally engaged in wholesale
trade increased by 25.9% (in current prices), this annual rate being
1.4 percentage points higher than in 2021 (Chart 2.12). Turnover
of enterprises whose main activity is market services provided to
enterprises increased in annual terms by 28.2% (in current prices)
in the same period, by 0.1 percentage points higher than in the
previous year.

Foreign trade
In 2022, the structure of foreign trade6 of the Republic of Moldova
has been strongly affected by changes in the context of the armed
conﬂict in Ukraine. Thereby, for the ﬁrst time, the phenomenon
of export of mineral products (essentially re-export of petroleum
products) to Ukraine in signiﬁcant quantities appears. At the same
6Quarterly data on the development of Moldova’s foreign trade, expressed in
thousands of US dollars, were used.

21
time, against the backdrop of a record agricultural harvest in 2021,
the export of agri-food products made an essential contribution to
the dynamics of total exports in the ﬁrst half of 2022. It should be
noted that against the backdrop of modest demand in 2022, the
annual rate of imports has moderated signiﬁcantly (after excluding
imports of mineral products).

Source: NBS, NBM calculations
The annual export rate in 2022 recorded a level of 37.9%, which is
10.4 percentage points higher than in 2021. The upward dynamics
of the annual export rate was driven by the development of exports
to the CIS countries (mainly Ukraine) and EU (Chart 2.13). In 2022,
the share of exports to the EU was about 58.6%, while those to CIS
countries and those classiﬁed as "Rest of the World" were 24.1%
and 17.3%, respectively.
By product category, the marked increase in the annual export rate
in 2022 was driven by the development of exports of "food, animal
products, beverages and fats" and "mineral products" (Chart 2.14).
The record agricultural harvest in 2021, overlapping with the
revival of external demand, has created favourable preconditions
for a sharp increase in agri-food exports in the ﬁrst half of 2022. At
the same time, the sharp increase in exports of mineral products
(re-export of petroleum products) was conditioned by the need to
compensate for the shortage of petroleum products in Ukraine
due to the disruption of maritime supply chains as a result of the
military conﬂict.
Analysing export trends by origin of products, it can be seen that
the acceleration of the annual export rate in 2022 was driven by
the increase in re-exports (Chart 2.15).
The annual rate of imports in 2022 recorded a level of 28.5% or
4.0 percentage points lower than in 2021. The moderation in the
annual rate of imports in 2022 was supported by developments
in imports from CIS countries and those classiﬁed as "Rest of the
World" (Chart 2.16). In the reporting year, imports from the EU
accounted for about 47.3%, while imports from the CIS and the
"Rest of the World" accounted for 23.7% and 29.0% respectively.

both the economically active population and the

Wage income
In 2022, the wage fund per economy increased by 15.7% compared
to the previous year’s level. Adjusted for CPI, it decreased by
9.9% compared to 2021. At the same time, the average number
of employees in the national economy showed modest growth,
increasing by only 0.2% compared to the previous year (Chart
2.21).

During the analysed period, average earnings in the national

sector of the economy, the average wage gain increased by 13.5%,
and in the real sector – by 15.9%. Wage earnings increased in all
economic activities. The most signiﬁcant increases were recorded
in accommodation and food service activities, information and
communication and transport and storage.
In 2022, in real terms, average earnings in the economy contracted
by 10.3% compared to 2021 (Chart 2.22). In the real sector it
contracted by 9.8% and in the public sector by 11.8%.

In 2022, the average annual rate CPI was 28.7%, 23.6 percentage
points higher than in 2021 (Table 2.1). During 2022, the annual
inﬂation rate continued the pronounced upward trajectory of the
previous year until October 2022. Thus, it increased from 13.9%
in December 2021 to 34.6% in October 2022. Subsequently, in
the last two months of the year, it followed a downward trend,
reaching 30.2% in December 2022. Therefore, during 2022 the
annual inﬂation rate was above the upper limit of the 5.0% ±1.5
percentage points range stipulated in the Medium-term monetary
policy strategy (Chart 2.23).
The acceleration in prices in the ﬁrst ten months of the year was
underpinned by rising international and regional market prices for
food and energy resources, which led to an increase in their
domestic prices, as well as the adjustment of several regulated
price tariffs (Chart 2.24). Therewith, the adjustment of tariffs, the
increase in fuel prices, but also the upward dynamics of wages
generated cost increases for economic agents that were gradually
reﬂected in prices.
The impact of the above factors was
signiﬁcantly ampliﬁed in the ﬁrst part of 2022 by the escalation of
the situation in Ukraine, which generated additional pressures on
food, fuel and some core inﬂation subcomponents (Chart 2.25).
Additional pressures on domestic food prices in the second part of
2022 were caused by the hydrological drought of the previous
summer.
At the same time, for some CPI subcomponents,
additional inﬂationary pressures were driven by the dynamics of
the MDL/USD exchange rate in the ﬁrst part of 2022.
Demand pressures observed during 2021 are estimated to have
eased in the ﬁrst part of 2022 and then to have eased inﬂationary
pressures generated by the above-mentioned factors. Towards the
end of 2022, the moderation in the annual CPI rate was supported
by the effect of a high base period at the end of 2021, subdued
demand from the population in the context of falling disposable
income of the population in real terms, and compensation to the
population for energy resources in the cold period of the year.
Core inﬂation8
In the ﬁrst ten months of 2022, the annual rate of core inﬂation has
been on an upward trajectory, increasing from 8.9% in December
2021 to 19.8% in October 2022. Subsequently, in the last two
months of the year it moderated to 18.5% in December 2022.
The upward trend in the ﬁrst part of the year was underpinned
by inﬂationary pressures on core inﬂation subcomponents, mainly
8Core inﬂation is calculated by the National Bureau of Statistics by excluding
prices outside the area inﬂuenced by monetary policy measures promoted by the
National Bank of Moldova, such as food and beverages, fuels, products and services
with regulated prices.


Chapter 2. Economy of the Republic of Moldova
25
driven by second-round effects associated with tariff adjustments,
rising commodity prices and the consequences of the military
conﬂict in Ukraine. The depletion of stocks of raw materials, either
of ﬁnal goods imported from Ukraine or of goods transiting this
country before reaching the Republic of Moldova (from Belarus,
Russia, Poland etc.), led to the search for new product suppliers,
the negotiation of new contracts, additional logistical costs. At the
same time, the increase in the annual core inﬂation rate, especially
in the ﬁrst part of the year, was also supported by the depreciation
of the national currency against the US dollar. In this regard, in
2022, the average ofﬁcial exchange rate of the MDL depreciated
by 6.9% against the US dollar.
Aggregate demand-related inﬂationary pressures observed in
2021 are estimated to have lost momentum. Thus, although the
wage bill grew strongly in 2022, the effect of this development
was signiﬁcantly eroded by rising inﬂation. A similar situation was
also observed for two other important sources of consumption
ﬁnancing,
remittances
and
social
beneﬁts,
whose
upward
dynamics were insufﬁcient to offset price increases and generate
additional demand for goods and services. At the same time, new
lending to individuals showed a downward dynamic in the context
of tighter credit conditions. The subdued demand therefore partly
mitigated the impact of the above-mentioned factors.
In structure, during 2022, the most signiﬁcant contributions to
core inﬂation were generated by the subcomponents "clothing",
"public alimentation", "means of transport, auto parts", "furniture",
"construction materials", "daily household maintenance" (Chart
2.26).

The prices of "clothing",
"means of transport,
auto parts",
"furniture", "construction materials" have shown a pronounced
upward trend in the context of rising raw material prices on the
international and regional markets. Therewith, prices were also
affected by higher transport costs. Additional pressures on the
local market were exerted by the depreciation of the national
currency against the US dollar.
Price increases in restaurants, cafes and canteens contributed to
the higher contribution from the public alimentation segment, as
a result of higher food prices, but also higher prices for utilities
(gas, electricity and heating). These, together with the increase in
wages, led to higher fees for higher education, fees for pre-school
education (nurseries and kindergartens), private tuition fees for
primary education, which also resulted in a positive contribution
from the education and training segment to core inﬂation.
The increase in prices of detergents, cleaning solutions for daily
household maintenance, household chemicals has led to an
increase in the contribution of the "daily household maintenance"
segment.
Within the sub-component "other", the increase in
payments for renting apartments can be highlighted, as a result of
the large in ﬂux of refugees from Ukraine, which increased the
demand for housing.

upward trend, increasing by 14.3 percentage points to 31.8% in

in contributions from the groups "milling and bakery products",
"fresh fruits", "meat, prepared and preserved meat", "beverages",
"milk and dairy products" and "vegetables" (Chart 2.27). Thereby,
the hydrological drought in the summer of 2022, the increase in
transport costs, the increase in production costs (higher gas and
electricity prices), the depreciation of the MDL/USD exchange rate,

pro-inﬂationary effects associated with the military conﬂict in
Ukraine, have created preconditions for a sharp increase in food
prices in 2022.
It should be noted that during the last four months of 2022, a
reversal of the upward trend in the annual rate of food prices
has emerged. This dynamic was inﬂuenced by both external and
internal factors.
Thereby, the moderation, in the second half
of 2022, of inﬂationary pressures through the channel of import
prices (Chart 2.28), together with the demand deﬁcit recorded in
the Moldovan economy have created preconditions for reversing
the upward trend in the annual rate of food prices.
Regulated prices
The upward trend in the annual rate of regulated prices since 2021
continued in 2022, increasing from 17.0% in January 2022 to 68.7%
in October 2022. Subsequently, in the last two months of 2022,
the annual rate of regulated prices followed a downward path,
reaching 55.7% in December 2022 (Chart 2.29). The increase in

regulated prices during 2022 was underpinned by the increase in
international and regional energy prices which was ampliﬁed by
the military conﬂict in Ukraine.
A signiﬁcant contribution to the dynamics of regulated prices
during 2022 was generated by the dynamics of network gas prices.
In this regard, ANRE, on 28 January 20229, has set a new tariff
for mains gas, with effect from 1 January 2022. As a result, the
price of a cubic metre of gas for domestic consumers has been
set at MDL 15.18 (with VAT). It should be noted that the tariff was
partially compensated by the government during the cold season.
Thus, for the ﬁrst 50 cubic metre the tariff was MDL 6.8, and the
next 100 cubic metre – MDL 7.910. Therefore, this impact has been
temporarily mitigated. Similarly, on 28 January 2022, the central
heating tariffs with effect from 1 January 2022 were also revised11.
Similarly, to the gas tariff in the network, the tariffs were partly
covered by the government12.

Chapter 2. Economy of the Republic of Moldova
27
As of April 2022, the upward trajectory of regulated prices has
become more pronounced, largely due to the full reﬂection of new
tariffs in the context of the end of the compensation period13.
Thereby, in April 2022, the NBS reﬂected the remaining impact of
the
late
2021
and
early
2022
tariff
adjustment
on
the
corresponding CPI subcomponents (natural gas – 41.6%, central
heating – 59.8% and heat – 62.9%).
Therewith, a signiﬁcant impact on regulated prices in the second
quarter of 2022 was also determined by the adjustment of the
electricity tariff from 1 April 2022. Thereby, ANRE approved new
tariffs for electricity supplied by I.C.S. "Premier Energy" S.R.L. in
the amount of MDL 2.17/kWh, 43.7% more than previous prices,
and for that supplied by S.A. "Furnizarea Energiei Electrice Nord" in
the amount of MDL 2.51/kWh for ﬁnal consumers, 22.9% more than
previous prices14. As a result, in the CPI, countrywide electricity
prices increased by 34.9% in April 2022.
Subsequently, over the summer, natural gas and electricity tariffs
were further increased. Thus, from 1 June 2022, the tariff for gas
delivered to household consumers was set at MDL 18.62 (with
VAT), 22.66% higher than the previous tariff15. On 8 June 202216
the electricity tariff was also revised. The new tariff was set in
the amount of 286 bani/kWh for S.A. "Furnizarea Energiei Electrice
Nord" (14.0% higher than the previous one) and 264 bani/kWh
(21.6% higher than the previous one) for consumers connected to
I.C.S. "Premier Energy" S.R.L. Therewith, according to the National
Agency for Energy Regulation ruling of 4 August, the natural gas
tariff for domestic consumers was adjusted to MDL 22.99/cubic
metre (with VAT), from MDL 18.62/cubic metre (with VAT).
A signiﬁcant impact on regulated prices in 2022 was also
determined by tariffs for transport services. In March-April 2022, a
number of price adjustments were made to urban travel prices in
the centre and north of the republic (Ungheni Town17, Balti Town18
and Soroca Town19). The beginning of the third quarter of 2022
was marked by the adjustment of public transport fares in
Chisinau Municipality20,
which led to an increase in urban
transport prices in the CPI by about 70.0%. Thus, the fare for
trolleybus trips increased from MDL 2 to MDL 6, and for bus and
minibus trips – from MDL 3 to MDL 6. The price of public transport
passes has also been increased.

28
Annual Report (NBM, 2022)
Intercity transport fares during the year showed an upward
dynamic in the context of rising prices for petroleum products,
which are more pronounced in the ﬁrst part of 2022. The ﬁrst
signs of adjustment of intercity fares appeared as early as the end
of 2021 and materialised as a result of negotiations between
representatives
of
service
providers
and
the
authorities
responsible for approving the new fares, valid from 1 March
202221. However, under the pressure of the continuous increase
in the prices of petroleum products, provision No. 9 of 10 March
2022 was issued, which provided for a derogation from the
provisions of the previously approved Methodology for calculating
fares for passenger and luggage transport services by car. This
allowed transport operators during the state of emergency to
modify fares for regular services in district and inter-district trafﬁc
up to the ceiling calculated according to the formulas in Annex 1
to this provision22. As a result, this prices segment has changed
several times, starting with the developments of petroleum
product prices, recording a maximum increase in March (by
34.8%).
The upward trajectory of the annual rate of regulated prices
observed during 2022 peaked in October 2022, after which it
followed a downward trend until the end of the year. This dynamic
was driven, on the one hand, by the further adjustment of tariffs
and,
on
the
other
hand,
by
the
implementation
of
the
compensation package granted by the state for the payment of
energy bills23 which temporarily mitigated the effect of the price
increase.
In this regard, according to the decision of 23 September 2022, the
ANRE increased the natural gas tariff to MDL 29.27/cubic meter
(with VAT)24, which led to a 27.3% increase in this subcomponent
of the CPI in October. Taking into account the compensation offered
by the state, natural gas prices in the CPI in November decreased
by about 49.0%. At the same time, the tariff for thermal energy
delivered to consumers by S.A. "Termoelectrica" was set at MDL
2,854/Gcal (excluding VAT), compared to the previous tariff of MDL
2,169/Gcal (excluding VAT), for that supplied by S.A. "Comgaz-
Plus" in the amount of MDL 3,766/Gcal (excluding VAT), compared
to the previous tariff of MDL 2,718/Gcal (excluding VAT), and for
that supplied to consumers by S.A. "CET-Nord" in the amount of
MDL 3,052/Gcal (excluding VAT), compared to the previous tariff of
MDL 2,115/Gcal (excluding VAT) with effect from 1 October 202225.
Adjustments to heating and hot water were reﬂected by the NBS in
the CPI during the fourth quarter of 2022, also taking into account
the effect of compensation for these services.
During the quarter, there were also some adjustments to the
electricity tariff. It increased from MDL 2.64/kWh (Premier Energy)
and MDL 2.86/kWh (S.A. "Furnizarea Energiei Electrice Nord") to
MDL 5.91/kWh and MDL 5.60/kWh, respectively26. Subsequently,
on 8 December, the National Agency for Energy Regulation lowered

Chapter 2. Economy of the Republic of Moldova

electricity tariffs. The main reason behind the decision was the new
electricity procurement price, following the signing on 3 December
of the contract between S.A. Energocom and MGRES. Thus, as
of 8 December, consumers in central and southern Moldova paid
MDL 4.62/kWh, and residents of the north – MDL 4.65/kWh27. The
adjustments were reﬂected by the NBS during the fourth quarter
of 2022, also taking into account the effect of compensation for
this service.
In the context of the continuous increase in energy tariffs, as well
as the increase in other costs, tariffs for water and sewage services
(Cahul, Soroca, Comrat) and sanitation services (Comrat, Cahul,
Chisinau) were adjusted during 2022.
At the same time, during 2022, positive but smaller contributions
from medicine and healthcare prices to the dynamics of regulated
prices were observed.

In the ﬁrst half of 2022, the annual rate of fuel and lubricants
prices was characterised by a pronounced upward dynamic, then,
starting in August, it reversed. Thus, the annual rate increased
from 29.3% in December 2021 to a level of 66.9% in July 2022
(Chart 2.30).
This dynamic has been supported, in particular, by the increase in
lubricants prices as a result of the considerable rise in international
and regional oil prices. Thus, the average oil price (Brent) in the
ﬁrst half of 2022 amounted to USD 105.9/barrel, 50.3% higher
than the average oil price in 2021. Subsequently, amid the decline
in oil prices (in the second part of 2022), the average price of
Brent crude oil amounted to USD 93.8/barrel, the annual rate of
fuel and lubricants prices decreased to 35.9% in December 2022.
The upward trend in lubricants prices on the domestic market was
also signiﬁcantly ampliﬁed by the conﬂict in the region.

During 2022, wood prices generated more pronounced positive
contributions to fuel and lubricants price developments compared
to 2021. The upward dynamics of coal prices on the international
market and the considerable increase in the import price of natural
gas led to higher coal and wood prices on the domestic market
during the reference year. The upward dynamics of the annual
rate of fuel and lubricants prices in 2022 was also supported by
the development of liqueﬁed gases prices.
Industrial prices
In the ﬁrst ten months of 2022, the annual rate of industrial prices
showed a marked upward trend, and in the last two months of the
year it reversed its trend. Thus, in December 2022, the annual
industrial price rate was 27.6%, 11.5 percentage points higher
than in December 2021. It should be noted that during 2022 the
annual rate of prices for products delivered on the domestic market
was at a higher level than that of prices for products delivered on
the foreign market (Chart 2.31).

An analysis of the development of the annual industrial price rate
by main branches shows that the high rates recorded during 2022
were supported by developments in the manufacturing and
energy sectors (Chart 2.32). Prices in the energy sector have risen
signiﬁcantly in 2022 against the backdrop of the global energy
crisis. At the same time, as a result of the considerable increase in
energy and raw material prices, prices in the manufacturing
industry also reacted.
Thus, in the context of the signiﬁcant
increase in cost components, the annual rate of prices in the
manufacturing industry increased substantially to 21.7% in
December 2022. The increase during 2022 in the annual rate of
prices in manufacturing was mostly driven by price increases in
the food industry, the manufacture of other non-metallic mineral
products28 and the manufacture of beverages.
Therewith, in
December 2022, the annual rate of prices in the extractive
industry recorded a level of 32.3%, generating a contribution of
0.7 percentage points to the price dynamics in the industry.

During 2022, the annual rate of prices in construction recorded a
pronounced upward trend. Thus, in the fourth quarter of 2022 it
stood at 21.3%, 17.0 percentage points higher than in the fourth

Producer’s prices for agricultural products
In 2022, producer prices of agricultural products increased by
13.6% compared to the previous year, due to increases in prices of
animal and crop products of 16.0% and 13.2%, respectively (Chart
2.34).
Developments in crop prices were mainly driven by increases in
prices of "ﬁeld and ﬁeld vegetables" (55.2%), "sugar beet" (46.7%),
"potatoes" (46.4%), "wheat" (28.4%) and "grain legumes" (23.9%).
Chart
2.34:
Annual prices rate of producer’s
agricultural products (%)
Source: NBS
It should be noted that in 2022 the agricultural harvest was
severely affected by the hydrological drought during the summer
months, thus, against the background of a reduced supply, the
prices of crop products increased considerably. Therewith, higher
production costs (fertilisers, diesel, seeds, spare parts, transport
costs) also contributed to the increase in crop prices.
The increase in prices for livestock products was driven by the
rise in prices for all product categories. Prices for eggs, meat (live
mass) and milk increased by 26.4%, 14.9% and 13.5% respectively.
It should be noted that the decrease in the volume of livestock
production for the sixth consecutive year contributed to the steady
decrease in the supply of livestock products on the domestic
market. As a consequence, the decrease in the domestic supply
of livestock products, overlapping with the increase in production
costs, inﬂuenced the upward dynamics of producer prices of
livestock products.

region, the annual CPI rate in the Republic of Moldova started
its upward trajectory faster and more pronounced already at the
end of 2021, compared to countries such as Romania, Ukraine,
Hungary, Poland. However, towards the end of 2022, the gap
between inﬂation in the Republic of Moldova (30.2%) and that
in Ukraine (26.6%) and Hungary (24.5%) narrowed signiﬁcantly
(Chart 2.35).
The annual CPI rate in Romania and Poland grew more slowly
and registered 16.4% and 16.6%, respectively, at the end of 2022.
Therewith, the annual inﬂation rate in Türkiye recorded signiﬁcantly
higher values than in the above-mentioned countries, however,
recording a moderation in December 2022 (64.4%). Lower levels
of inﬂation towards the end of 2022 in the countries – main trading
partners of the Republic of Moldova were recorded in the Russian
Federation (11.9%), the Euro area (9.2%) and China (1.8%).
It is worth mentioning some objective preconditions that favoured
higher inﬂation in the Republic of Moldova compared to inﬂation in
the countries – main trading partners:
• High
share
of
food
expenditure
in
household
expenditure in Moldova.
The pronounced inﬂationary
pressures in the region over the past two years have been
largely driven by rising commodity prices, mainly food and
energy prices, associated with the easing of COVID-19 –
related restrictive measures, deteriorating supply chains and
stimulative monetary policies. Given that the share of food
prices in the Republic of Moldova is higher than in other
countries, and given the open nature of the economy, the
increase in food prices on the international and regional
markets was reﬂected more rapidly and more pronounced on
the local market.
• Vulnerability of vegetable and fruit prices on the local
market to agro-meteorological conditions. Pronounced
increase in food prices, especially for fruits and vegetables,
in the second part of 2022 was also accentuated by the dry
conditions of the previous summer.
• Lack of own energy resources and dependence on
imports. In contrast to the Russian Federation, Romania or
Ukraine, the Republic of Moldova does not have its own
resources of oil and natural gas and, therefore, the trend of
increasing prices of energy resources on the international
market, ampliﬁed by the conﬂict in Ukraine, will be fully
reﬂected in the prices of these products on the local market.
The cold season compensation granted by the state only
partially delays this phenomenon.


32
Annual Report (NBM, 2022)
• Existence of price cap mechanisms for a longer period
in some countries. Inﬂation in some trading partners, such
as Romania for example, has been signiﬁcantly dampened
by capping schemes for gas and electricity prices. Without
them, inﬂation at the end of 2022 would have been about 11
percentage points higher than at present29.
• Imbalances created by postponing the adjustment of
some tariffs in due course. Another factor that favoured
the pronounced acceleration of inﬂation in the last two years
in the Republic of Moldova could be the postponement/delay
of the adjustment of some tariffs during the last years for
social/political reasons,
although there were economic
preconditions
for
their
revision,
which
led
to
the
accumulation of considerable ﬁnancial gaps that required a
more pronounced adjustment in 2021-2022.
• Different monetary policy objectives determined by
the different structure and level of development of
some economies. It should not be overlooked that different
countries have different quantitative monetary policy targets.
For example, the European Central Bank has an inﬂation
target of 2.0% in the Euro area and calibrates its instruments
accordingly, while the inﬂation target set by the NBM is 5.0%
±1.5 percentage points.
• Different phases of the economic cycle. In some regions,
such as China, during 2022, restrictions imposed by the
COVID-19 pandemic were still in place, which signiﬁcantly
affected aggregate demand and sustained such a low level
of inﬂation (Chart 2.37).
• High
presence
of
imported
goods
from/through
Ukraine
in
goods
consumed
in
the
Republic
of
Moldova until the situation in the region escalated.
Substitution of products from Ukraine (which were abundant
on the Moldovan market and were the most affordable by
price/quality in the region) by similar products but from
another
(more
expensive)
country
of
origin
had
a
considerable impact on the CPI. At the same time, the import
of many products was carried out through the Moldovan
border with Ukraine and the Odessa seaport, but the use of
alternative routes increased transport costs.
29Inﬂation Report, February 2023,
https://www.bnr.ro/PublicationDocuments.aspx?icid=3922


Chapter 2. Economy of the Republic of Moldova
33
2.4
Public sector and ﬁscal policy
Implementing the national public budget
In 2022, national public budget indicators were affected by global,
regional and national risks and uncertainties stemming from the
tense situation due to the war in Ukraine, the global energy crisis,
the continuous increase in international commodity, food and
energy prices, inﬂationary pressures and economic contraction.
As a result, revenues increased at a slower annual pace and
public expenditure increased at a faster pace, the budget deﬁcit
amounted to MDL 8,868.6 million. Decreased economic activity,
lower domestic demand, increased transfers, and changes in the
income of both the population and economic agents affected
the revenues collected in taxes and duties for the state budget.
Thus, according to the data provided by the Ministry of Finance,
the revenues of the national public budget were accumulated in
the amount of MDL 91,505.4 million, 18.3% above the level of
accumulated revenues in 2021 (Chart 2.38). Relative to GDP data,
Chart 2.38: National public budget indicators
Source: Ministry of Finance, NBM calculations
revenue collected in 2022 was 33.6%, up 1.6 percentage points
from the previous year.
The major part of the budget revenue was accumulated from taxes
and duties, which had a share of 62.9% (MDL 57,524.2 million).
Contributions and compulsory insurance premiums accounted for
27.2% (MDL 24,923.3 million), while grants and other revenues
accounted for 5.0% (MDL 4,539.4 million) and 4.9% (MDL 4,518.6
million) of total budget revenue respectively.
During the reporting year, the Republic of Moldova received
ﬁnancial support for budget support in the form of external grants
in the amount of MDL 4,539.4 million or 85.5% more than in 2021.
Relative to GDP, grants accounted for 1.7%, 0.7 percentage points
more than in 2021.
The assistance provided by international
ﬁnancial organisations and donor countries is an important
contribution to the economic recovery and implementation of the
reforms needed for the modernisation and recovery of the
Republic of Moldova.
Therewith, budget expenditure has been increased to provide
social security and support for the population. As a result, the
national public budget expenditure in the reference year totalled
MDL 100,374.0 million, an increase of 22.4% compared to 2021.
Of the total expenditure, the largest share was allocated to social
protection (38.2%), 15.8% to education, and 13.6% to health care.
Public expenditure as a share of GDP was 36.8% or 2.9 percentage
points higher than in 2021.
It should be noted that during 2022, transfers from the state
budget (SB) to the state social insurance budget, (SSIB), the
mandatory health insurance fund (MHIF) and local budgets (LB)
were made in the amount of MDL 38,056.8 million (an increase
of 22.1% compared to 2021). The major share of transfers was
directed to local budgets, about 45.1% of total transfers, followed
by transfers to SSIB, about 38.9%, with the MHIF receiving a more
modest 16.0%.


34
Annual Report (NBM, 2022)
In this context, in 2022, the execution of the national public budget
resulted in a deﬁcit of MDL 8,868.6 million. In relation to GDP,
this deﬁcit represents 3.3%, 1.3 percentage points above the
2021 level. The sources of ﬁnancing of the national public deﬁcit
consisted of net external borrowing in the amount of MDL 13,159.5
million, which represents 4.8% of GDP (up 1.4 percentage points
from the previous year) and of changes in balances in the amount
of MDL 5,529.0 million or 2.0% of GDP (up 4.3 percentage points
from the previous year). Therewith, the balances of funds in the
accounts of the component budgets of the national public budget
at the end of the year amounted to MDL 6,337.1 million.

According to preliminary data, in 2022, the balance of payments
current account of the Republic of Moldova recorded a deﬁcit
of USD 2,275.2 million, which increased by 33.9% compared to
that recorded in 2021. Relative to GDP, the current account was
-15.8% (compared to -12.4% in 2021) (Chart 2.39, Table A.1). This
dynamic was driven by an increase in the external trade deﬁcit in
goods as well as a decrease in the primary income surplus, while
the services balance increased substantially, but insufﬁcient to
cover the negative impact mentioned.
The deﬁcit on foreign trade in goods31 recorded in the balance of
payments amounted to USD 4,983.6 million, increasing by 18.9%
due to a higher increase in imports of goods (USD +1,933.2 million)
than in exports (USD +1,139.8 million). The ratio of the deﬁcit
balance of external trade in goods to GDP was 34.6% (compared
to -30.6% in 2021).
Exports of goods recorded in the balance of payments (excluding
goods after/ for processing) totalled USD 3,702.0 million (+44.5%
compared to 2021).
In the reporting year, 54.1% of goods exports went to the EU-
2732, 28.7% went to the CIS and the rest went to other countries.
Signiﬁcant contributions to export growth came from exports to
the CIS, which increased by 2.2 times compared to 2021, and
exports to the EU-27, which increased by 40.9%, while exports to
other countries decreased (Chart 2.40).
Agri-food products continued to be the main category of exported
goods (54.2% of the total), in 2022 their exports increased by
35.6% compared to 2021, mainly due to the increase in exports of
sunﬂower oil, which increased by 3.1 times (USD +249.3 million),
corn – by 2.7 times (USD +212.6 million) and sunﬂower seeds – by
70.9% (USD +139.6 million). Exports of mineral products recorded
the largest increase – by USD 586.5 million (22.9 times), largely
due to the re-export of petroleum and bituminous mineral oils to
Ukraine.
Imports of goods recorded in the balance of payments (excluding
goods after/for processing) amounted to USD 8,685.7 million
(+28.6% compared to 2021). There was an increase in imports

from all geographical areas. Thereby, imports of goods from the

from the CIS – by 7.7%, making up 24.7% of the total value, and
from other countries increased by 25.5% to 18.3% of the total
(Chart 2.41).
