Imports of mineral products increased by 2.4 times compared to
2021, both due to higher petroleum product prices and physical
volumes imported for some mineral product categories. It should
be noted that a signiﬁcant share of imported mineral products,
especially
diesel
and
automotive
gasoline,
was
exported
(re-exported) to Ukraine. Imports of agri-food products increased
by 27.8%.
Imports of energy products and electricity in 2022 amounted to
USD 2,541.7 million, a 2.4-fold increase compared to 2021, due to
price increases on international markets and reorientation towards
new suppliers. In 2022, the most signiﬁcant increase was seen
in the import of diesel fuel – 2.5 times, natural gas (in gaseous
state) – 2.1 times, automotive gasoline – 2.4 times, coal – 2.3
times, import of electricity increased to USD 152.6 million (Chart
2.42).
The foreign trade surplus with services increased by 91.4% from
2021 to USD 904.1 million. This development was driven by a more
pronounced increase in services exports (USD +640.0 million) than
in imports (USD +208.2 million).
The ratio of the balance of services to GDP was 6.3%, increasing
from the 2021 level (Chart 2.43).
Within
exports,
travel
services
made
the
biggest
positive
contribution. Within travel services, personal travel increased by
2.1 times and business travel – by 13.9%. Exports of transport
services increased by 53.7%, and IT services – by 32.6%. The
increase in imports of services resulted from the increase in
imports of transport services (+33.2%, especially freight transport
which increased by 17.2%) and travel (+17.3% compared to
2021).
In 2022, the primary revenue surplus decreased by 4.1 times
compared to 2021 and totalled USD 65.5 million, constituting
0.5% of GDP (Chart 2.44).
This decrease was driven by an
increase in outﬂows (USD +188.6 million), while primary income
inﬂows decreased (USD -12.1 million).
Thus, compensation of
resident
employees
for
work
performed
for
non-resident
employers decreased by 6.1% to USD 834.0 million, and direct
investment income payments increased by 39.3%.
Secondary income saw a decrease in the surplus by 0.8% from
2021 to USD 1,738.9 million (12.1% of GDP) (Chart 2.45). Personal
remittances received from abroad by Moldovan residents declined
by 2.3% and amounted to USD 1,203.3 million. The net value of
technical assistance, humanitarian aid, and grants received under
international cooperation by all institutional sectors increased by
61.0% in 2022 and amounted to USD 563.3 million.
Personal remittances33 received by residents of the Republic
of Moldova amounted to USD 1,985.8 million (-3.6% compared to
2021), which represents 13.8% relative to GDP.
33Personal remittances are an additional item in the balance of payments and
reﬂect the sum of net compensation of employees (excluding workers’ expenses
incurred in the host country and some taxes and duties paid by residents abroad),
personal and capital transfers between households.

Public debt
At the end of 2022, the public debt of the Republic of Moldova
amounted to MDL 94,660.0 million, representing 34.7% of GDP.
The public debt increased by MDL 16,907.3 million, or 21.7%,
at the end of 2022, due to the increase in external public debt
recalculated in national currency (with a positive contribution of
20.2 percentage points) and domestic public debt (with a positive
contribution of 1.5 percentage points). As a share of GDP, domestic
public debt amounted to 12.7% and external debt – 22.5% (Chart
2.53).
Chart 2.53: Public debt as a share of GDP (%, end of
the year)
Source: Ministry of Finance, NBS, NBM calculations
As of 31 December 2022, public debt consisted of 63.6% external
public debt and 36.4% domestic public debt.
The balance of external public debt, denominated in foreign
currency, as of 31 December 2022 increased by 25.3% compared
to 31 December 2021 and amounted to USD 3,140.6 million.
External public debt, as a value expressed in MDL, totalled MDL
60,167.6 million at the end of the reporting year, an increase of
35.3% compared to the end of 2021.
Domestic public debt at the end of 2022 amounted to MDL
34,492.4 million, 3.6% higher than at the end of 2021.
The
increase in domestic public debt was driven by additional
issuance of SS on the primary market by 7.5%. At the same time,
in 2022,
SS issued in 2016 for the execution of payment
obligations derived from public guarantees were redeemed in the
amount of MDL 290.0 million.
Thus, at the end of 2022, the
domestic debt was made up of SS issued on the primary market
(58.5%), SS issued for the execution of public guarantees (35.0%)
and converted SS (6.5%).
In 2022, funds amounting to MDL 2,645.4 million were used to
service the public debt from the national public budget (39.4%
more than in 2021). External public debt servicing amounted to
MDL 560.6 million (48.3% more compared to 2021), and domestic
public debt servicing – MDL 2,084.8 million (37.2% more compared
to 2021).

42
Annual Report (NBM, 2022)
Chapter 3
Monetary policy
3.1
Monetary policy objective
According to the provisions of Law No. 548/1995 on the National
Bank of Moldova, the NBM is the central bank of the Republic of
Moldova, being an autonomous public legal entity that establishes
and promotes monetary and foreign exchange policy in the state.
During 2022, the NBM operated in accordance with the provisions
of the Medium-term monetary policy strategy approved by the
Council of Administration of the National Bank of Moldova on 27
December 2012.
According to the strategy, in order to ensure and maintain price
stability, the NBM has set the continuous annual inﬂation target,
measured by the consumer price index published monthly by
the National Bureau of Statistics (NBS), at 5.0% with a possible
deviation of ±1.5 percentage points.
During 2022, the National Bank of Moldova promoted a tightening
monetary policy with the aim of moderating the rapid growth rates
of consumer prices, mitigating the side effects of supply shocks,
stimulating ﬁnancial intermediation in the national currency, and
encouraging savings at the expense of consumption. This policy
also aimed to balance the trade deﬁcit and anchor inﬂationary
expectations.
Therewith, the magnitude of monetary policy measures was
adjusted towards the end of the year,
focusing on easing
measures.
This adjustment was made in response to the
anticipation of a reversal in the annual inﬂation trajectory after its
peak
in
October
2022
and
the
emergence
of
signiﬁcant
disinﬂationary pressures stemming from reduced aggregate
demand, amid a negative GDP deviation.
3.2
Monetary policy decisions
The conduct of monetary policy during 2022 was inﬂuenced by
major risks and uncertainties regarding consumer price stability
and inﬂationary pressures.
These factors conditioned the
maintenance of a tight monetary policy stance. The monetary
policy
measures
taken
were
appropriate
from
several
perspectives. They aimed at mitigating the second-round effects
of external price shocks, balancing aggregate demand, promoting
saving over immediate consumption, and easing pressures on the
depreciation of the domestic currency caused by the widening
current account deﬁcit and capital outﬂow.


Chapter 3. Monetary policy
43
The monetary policy promoted by the NBM has created monetary
conditions likely to mitigate the alert growth rates of consumer
prices and the side effects of supply-side shocks, being aimed at
anchoring inﬂation expectations and bringing inﬂation back within
±1.5 percentage points of the inﬂation target of 5.0% over the
medium term.
During 2022, nine meetings of the Executive Board of the National
Bank of Moldova were held on monetary policy promotion. Eight
meetings were held according to the approved schedule and one
extraordinary meeting. In the context of the gradual adjustment
of the anti-inﬂationary monetary policy stance and with a view to
supporting the timely consistency of monetary policy instruments
from the perspective of the fundamental objective, the Executive
Board of the National Bank of Moldova amended the calendar of
meetings of the Executive Board on monetary policy promotion
ﬁve times during 2022.
At the meetings of the Executive Board of the National Bank of
Moldova on the promotion of monetary policy during 2022, six
decisions to increase, two decisions to maintain and one decision
to decrease the base rate applied to the main short-term monetary
policy operations and the rates on overnight deposit and credit
standing facilities were taken.
At the same time, the Executive Board of the National Bank of
Moldova took decisions on the required reserve ratio from the funds
attracted in MDL and non-convertible currency, four decisions to
increase, four decisions to maintain and one decision to decrease.
Three decisions to increase and six decisions to maintain the
reserve requirement in freely convertible foreign currency were
taken.
During 2022, global, regional and national risks and uncertainties
have been heightened by the tense situation due to the war in
Ukraine, the global energy crisis, the continued rise in international
commodity, food and energy prices, and disruptions in supply,
production and transport chains, and the outlook for economic
conditions in the context of these events has been precarious.
Chart
3.1:
Development
of
monetary
policy
instruments
Source: NBM
In these circumstances, central banks revised their inﬂation
forecasts upwards for the coming periods and, in response,
maintained an anti-inﬂationary monetary policy. The regional and
international
context
has
unbalanced
the
domestic
macroeconomic environment and put pressure on inﬂation
through the import channel. Compared to other countries, the
Republic of Moldova is more dependent on quotations and
developments on international markets, which makes our country
even more vulnerable to price shocks.
Thus, during 2022, the National Bank of Moldova undertook
measures to tighten monetary policy and, in this context, the
Executive Board of the NBM decided to gradually increase the
monetary policy interest rate from 6.50% in December 2021 to
21.50% annually in December 2022 (Chart 3.1). The aim of raising
the base rate was to create the monetary conditions necessary to
moderate the growth rates of consumer prices. At the same time,
the required reserve ratio of the funds attracted in MDL was
increased during 2022 from 26.0% to 40.0%.
At the meeting of the Executive Board of the NBM held on 5
December 2022, the decision to resume the monetary policy


44
Annual Report (NBM, 2022)
easing measures, to lower the base rate by 1.5 percentage points
to
20.0%,
was
approved,
which
was
conditional
on
the
conﬁrmation of the veracity of the forecast of the National Bank of
Moldova and its associated disinﬂationary trend. This decision was
taken in the context of the prospect of a reversal of the annual
inﬂation trajectory after the peak in the fourth quarter of 2022
and the emergence of pronounced disinﬂationary pressures from
aggregate demand amid a negative GDP deviation.
Although the risks and uncertainties to inﬂation were persistent
and directly related to the way the events associated with the
military conﬂict in Ukraine unfolded, including its implications for
the global and domestic macroeconomic environment, the NBM’s
decision came to support a comprehensive outlook on economic
activity, shaping the creation of preconditions on the money,
credit and foreign exchange markets necessary to revive domestic
aggregate demand, which, within the limits of the transmission
mechanism, were propagated through several channels, including
credit.
Therewith, the decision to reduce the reserve requirement ratio of
MDL and non-convertible foreign currency from 40.0% to 34.0%,
by 3.0 percentage points in December 2022 and by 3.0 percentage
points in January 2023, led to additional liquidity in the money
market, contributing to an increase in credit resources for the real
and public sectors.
The outlook for 2023 is linked to the continuation of monetary
policy easing measures, which are conditional on the conﬁrmation
of the veracity of the latest forecast of the National Bank of
Moldova
and
its
associated
disinﬂationary
trend.
Recent
information generally points to the main assumptions, conclusions
and assumptions published in the reports of the National Bank of
Moldova,
the
slowdown
in
the
pace
of
inﬂation
growth,
expectations of appreciation pressures on the exchange rate,
combined with the decline in imports,
the deceleration of
household consumption, the decline in investment etc.
In this context, the Republic of Moldova, at the end of 2022, joined
the ﬁrst countries in the world to start monetary policy easing. This
was due to the fact that the inﬂationary process, compared to other
countries in the region, started earlier and the restrictive monetary
policy measures had the expected impact of moderating inﬂation.
Thereby, the NBM aimed to anchor inﬂation expectations, to bring
inﬂation within ±1.5 percentage points of the 5.0% medium-term
inﬂation target.
3.3
Monetary policy achievements
Money market operations, standing facilities, and the required
reserve
mechanism
continued
to
play
a
key
role
in
the
implementation of monetary policy. The NBM made extensive and
ﬂexible use of both the instruments designed to absorb excess
liquidity in the market and those intended to provide liquidity to
banks. This involved employing both money market operations
and standing facilities extended to banks.


Chapter 3. Monetary policy
45
Open market operations
The daily balance of the NBC was quite consistent in the ﬁrst two
months of 2022. However, the surplus quickly shrank thereafter,
and the banks’ net liquidity position then turned sharply negative
in the context of the increasing preference for liquidity, including
the massive interventions of the NBM as a seller on the foreign
exchange market in the ﬁrst quarter in a net volume of USD 439.2
million (about MDL 8,001.8 million), with the outbreak of the war
in Ukraine. Thereby, in March the NBM became a net creditor
Chart 3.2: Evolution of daily balance of open market
operations (MDL million)
Source: NBM
to the banking system for the ﬁrst time since 2015. As a result,
the balance of repo operations recorded an all-time high of MDL
3,894.0 million at the end of March. These operations were also
complemented by banks’ consistent use of the overnight credit
facility during this period.
Under the impact of the increased pace of the increase in the
required
reserve
ratio
for
banks’
liabilities
in
MDL
in
May-September, partly offset by net foreign exchange purchases
during this period, the NBC’s daily balance was pushed towards a
minimum value recorded at the end of the third quarter (MDL
1,057.9 million), and the balance of repo operations remained
high.
The return to a positive net liquidity position of banks in October
and the subsequent consolidation of its relatively high values
resulted in a signiﬁcant reduction in banks’ demand for liquidity
in the last two months of the year and, consequently, in a drastic
decrease in the number of participants and in the volume of
bids submitted in repo auctions, whose average daily stock was
considerably reduced compared to previous periods. Given the
absence of bids in the last two auctions, the balance of repo
transactions recorded at the end of 2022 was zero.
Against the backdrop of the NBM’s monetary policy decision in
early December and the signals sent by the central bank in this
context, as well as the resumption of the cycle of decreasing the
reserve requirement norm, the NBC fully recovered the decreases
recorded
during
the
year,
following
a
moderately
upward
trajectory, and recorded an annual maximum of MDL 6,598.6
million at the end of 2022 (Chart 3.2).
Issuance of NBM certiﬁcates
The NBM conducted NBC placement auctions on a weekly basis,
with a 14-day maturity and with the announcement of the
maximum interest rate (NBM base rate).
The volume of
certiﬁcates issued was considerably compressed (about 2 times),
a situation reﬂected in the annual average of the stock of NBC
placements, which decreased by MDL 1,949.1 million to MDL
2,576.4 million.
Repo operations
Repo operations were conducted on a weekly basis through the
no-ceiling tender procedure, for 14 days and at a ﬁxed rate (NBM
base rate plus a margin of 0.25 percentage points). The reduction
in liquidity levels in the money market was associated with an
increase in the banks’ use of repo operations. Thus, the volume of
liquidity delivered MDL 41,370.0 million) was clearly higher than
in the previous year (MDL 1,387.0 million).


46
Annual Report (NBM, 2022)
Standing facilities
The standing facility interest rate corridor system used by the NBM
during 2022 continued to signal the general stance of monetary
policy, helping to keep money market interest rates predominantly
within the rate corridor, while ensuring, along with money market
operations, an adequate level of short-term liquidity in the banking
system.
Interest rates on standing facilities continued the upward trend
that began in mid-2021, with six consistent rate increases (of 2.0
and 3.0 percentage points, respectively) over 2022 as a whole,
with the symmetric corridor around the base rate maintained at
±2.0 percentage points.
The peak of interest rates (23.50% on overnight loans and 19.50%
on overnight deposits) was reached in August, after which the NBM
stopped the monetary policy tightening cycle, keeping the corridor
rates at the same level until December, when the trend reversed.
In early December, the National Bank resorted to monetary easing
measures, lowering monetary policy rates by 1.50 percentage
points to 22.00% and 18.00% for overnight loans and overnight
deposits, respectively.
During 2022, banks drew on both standing facilities, predominantly
at the end of reserve maintenance periods.
Banks’ use of the deposit facility offered by the NBM in 2022
Chart 3.3: Evolution of average monthly balance of
overnight deposits/credits in 2019-2022 (MDL million)
Source: NBM
has increased compared to previous years, with banks traditionally
using the overnight deposit facility towards the end of reserve
maintenance periods as they meet their reserve maintenance
obligations.
The total volume of overnight deposits placed by banks with
the National Bank in 2022 amounted to MDL 110.9 billion (2021:
MDL 59.3 billion), with the average daily stock standing at MDL
429.9 million, almost double the level of MDL 219.1 million in the
previous year. The average monthly balance of banks’ overnight
placements recorded uneven values during the year, the maximum
(MDL 866.7 million) being recorded in November and the minimum
(MDL 282.1 million) in March (Chart 3.3).
As for the overnight credit facility, in 2022 for the ﬁrst time, after a
seven-year break (since 2014), banks borrowed so much from the
NBM via overnight loans, against the background of the military
conﬂict in Ukraine. The total amount of overnight loans granted by
the NBM in 2022 amounted to MDL 14,074.0 million, an increase of
more than 6.4 times compared to the previous year (MDL 2,191.5
million).
Unlike in previous years, when the credit facility was usually
requested at the end of the reserve requirement periods, in 2022
(during the ﬁrst months of the war in the neighbouring country)
banks drew on overnight credits from the NBM for the whole
months. In March alone, in the context of the liquidity shortage,
the NBM delivered almost half of the annual volume of overnight
loans – MDL 6,523.0 million, which is also the monthly maximum
recorded this year. Also in March 2022, the maximum balance of
overnight loans (MDL 366.5 million) was awarded.


Chapter 3. Monetary policy
47
Required reserves
During 2022, the NBM, in order to moderate inﬂationary pressures
and the dollarization phenomenon, through monetary policy
decisions has gradually adjusted the required reserves norms in
the direction of increasing them (Chart 3.4). In this regard, the
NBM increased by 15.0 percentage points in 5 steps the required
reserve ratio of funds attracted in freely convertible currency
(FCC) and in 6 steps the required reserve ratio of funds attracted
in MDL and non-convertible currency (MDL). In December, the
Chart 3.4: Evolution of required reserves in MDL and
foreign currency in 2019-2022 (%)
Source: NBM
Chart 3.5: Evolution of the attracted means subject
to reservation in the years 2019-2022 (averages per
observation period)
Source: NBM
latter was reduced by 3.0 percentage points amid the emergence
of pronounced disinﬂationary pressures.
Therewith, the NBM
continued
its
policy
of
recalibrating
reserve
requirements,
increasing the gap between the two norms to 8.0 percentage
points, with a view to encouraging the economy to switch to
ﬁnancing in domestic currency. Thereby, as of 31 December 2022,
the required reserves ratio in MDL amounted to 37.0%, that in
FCC – to 45.0%, compared to 26.0% and 30.0%, respectively, as
of 31 December 2021.
In this context, required reserves in MDL held by banks in the
period 16 December 2022 – 15 January 2023 amounted to MDL
20,153.6 million, up by 61.0% compared to the reserves held by
banks during 16 December 2021 – 15 January 2022, while the
funds attracted in MDL and non-convertible currencies (NCC) from
which they are constituted increased by 13.2% (Graﬁcul 3.5).
As of 31 December 2022, freely convertible currencies (FCC)
required reserves held by banks totalled USD 254.1 million and
EUR 605.9 million, increasing by 75.8% and 49.9% respectively
compared to 31 December 2021. As for the attracted funds subject
to reservation, those in US dollars increased by 17.2% and those
in EUR remained virtually unchanged.
According to Article 17 of Law No. 548/1995 on the National Bank
of Moldova, required reserves may be remunerated and, in
accordance with the related regulatory framework, if required
reserves are remunerated, interest shall be paid on the part
exceeding 5.0% of the liabilities on the basis of which required
reserves are established. During 2022, the NBM also implemented
a series of monetary policy rate increases, with the interest rate
on the NBM’s overnight deposit facility, from which required
reserves in MDL are remunerated, reaching 19.5% in August, and
subsequently being lowered to 18.0% in December. Under these
circumstances, the MDL reserve requirement remuneration rate
increased from 4.44% as of 31 December 2021 to 18.19% as of 31
December 2022. In the FCC it remained unchanged – at 0.01%.
These changes, as well as the increase in the balance of funds
attracted by banks, from which the required reserves are made
up, led to an increase in the NBM’s expenditure on interest on
required reserves by about 16.3 times compared to 2021, to the
amount of MDL 2,135.9 million.


48
Annual Report (NBM, 2022)
Interventions on the domestic foreign exchange market
In 2022, the National Bank of Moldova intervened on the domestic
foreign exchange market both as a buyer and seller of foreign
exchange for monetary policy purposes, in order to avoid excessive
ﬂuctuations of the MDL in the context of signiﬁcant surplus or
deﬁcit of foreign exchange on the intrabank market at different
times of the year.
During the period under review,
the volume of net sales
interventions of the National Bank of Moldova made on the
interbank foreign exchange market against MDL at the exchange
date amounted to USD 249.81 million (of which USD 192.18
million equivalent to EUR 184.50 million).
In addition, foreign
exchange
conversions
were
carried
out
with
World
Bank
institutions in the form of net purchases of USD 3.13 million (Chart
3.6).
Chart 3.6: Evolution of the ofﬁcial MDL/USD exchange
rate and the volume of daily transactions of the NBM in
2019-2022
Source: NBM
Therewith, during the reporting period, the NBM carried out selling
swap operations amounting to USD 121.88 million (equivalent to
EUR 120.00 million) and purchase swap operations amounting to
USD 49.73 million (of which USD 9.93 million, equivalent to EUR
9.00 million).


Chapter 3. Monetary policy
49
3.4
Monetary
and
foreign
exchange
conditions
Dynamic of monetary indicators
Chart 3.7: Evolution of M2 money supply components
(%, increase compared to the same month last year)
Source: NBM
Chart 3.8: Dynamics of the main components of M3
money supply (%, increase compared to the same
month last year)
Source: NBM
Chart 3.9: Average annual balance of demand against
the economy (%, annual growth)
Source: NBM
The development of monetary indicators during 2022 was
affected by the military conﬂict in Ukraine, the slowdown in
economic activity, high inﬂation rates, and excessive liquidity in
the money market.
Under these conditions, the monetary
aggregates recorded similar increases to the previous year. Thus,
the annual growth rate of broad money supply at the end of 2022
was 5.3% in annual terms.
The behaviour of all monetary
aggregates during the reference year was similar.
Money supply
Throughout 2022,
the ongoing military conﬂict in Ukraine
continuously impacted economic activity at both national and
regional levels. At the same time, the energy crisis, inﬂationary
developments in Moldova’s primary foreign trade partners, and
domestic inﬂationary pressures – fuelled by an increase in the
wage bill in the economy and cash transfers to individuals – had a
notable inﬂuence on Moldova’s money market. As a result, money
supply (M2) grew by 9.6% in 2022, 0.1 percentage points above
the annual growth rate in 2021 (Chart 3.7).
A more detailed
analysis of the M2 monetary aggregate shows a slowdown in
growth until August 2022, after which it remained on a positive
growth trend until the end of 2022, largely inﬂuenced by the
development of deposits in national currency. Both the deposits in
MDL and the "money in circulation" component showed the same
behaviour during 2022. Thus, at the end of 2022, deposits in
national currency and money in circulation increased compared to
2021 by 10.2% and 8.5%, respectively.
Developments in MDL deposits recorded an annual increase of
10.2% at the end of December (Chart 3.8), largely driven by the
increase in term deposits of individuals. Sight deposits in MDL
made a neutral contribution, virtually at the previous year’s level,
with an annual increase of -0.1%.
At the end of December 2022, money in circulation amounted
to MDL 34.4 billion, up by MDL 2.7 billion since the beginning of
the year. The annual growth rate of money in circulation since
the beginning of 2022 continued the downward trend since 2021,
reaching the minimum point of decrease of -5.3% per year in July
2022, after which the trend reversed and towards the end of 2022
it increased to 8.5% per year. During the year, on average, an
increase in cash turnover was observed compared to last year. The
most signiﬁcant contributions to the increase in cash in circulation
came from net ﬂows from current and deposit account transactions
and net ﬂows from foreign exchange transactions from individuals.
At the same time, money supply (M3) grew at an annual rate of
5.3%, 6.0 percentage points lower than in 2021 (Chart 3.8).
At the end of 2022, the growth rate of foreign currency deposits
was negative at -4.4%, driven by the contribution of both term and
sight deposits. Foreign currency term deposits recalculated in MDL
decreased by 5.5% annually and sight deposits decreased by 3.6%
annually.


50
Annual Report (NBM, 2022)
Chart 3.10:
Dynamics of the components of the
average annual demand balance in relation to the
economy (%, annual growth)
Source: NBM
Chart 3.11:
Dynamics of volume and weighted
average rates for new loans granted in MDL
Source: NBM
Chart 3.12: Dynamics of volume of new loans granted
in MDL (%, annual growth)
Source: NBM
At the end of 2022, the share of deposits in MDL in total deposits
amounted to 61.8%, increasing by 3.4 percentage points, and the
share of deposits in foreign currency – 38.2%, respectively.
In the structure of deposits in MDL, the share of term deposits
in total deposits in MDL at the end of December 2022 amounted
to 48.6%, increasing by 5.3 percentage points compared to the
end of December 2021. As for the structure of foreign currency
deposits, 43.6% represented term deposits, 0.5 percentage points
below the level recorded in 2021.
Lending market
After the upswing in 2021, the dynamics of the average annual
balance of claims on the economy35 in 2022 followed a downward
trend, as the average annual balance of the individuals’ component
decreased (Chart 3.9). The average annual balance of individuals’
claims on the economy grew at an annual rate, in nominal terms,
of 22.5%, up from 29.0% in 2021. The decline in the balance was
largely driven by a decrease in the domestic currency component,
as the volume of new loans granted by licensed banks declined
amid the upward trend in related interest rates. At the same time,
the average annual balance of demand on the economy of legal
entities, whose share amounted to 59.0%, increased slightly.
The annual growth rate of the average annual balance of demand
against the economy in national currency decreased compared
to 2021 (Chart 3.10). Thus, the average annual balance of the
component in MDL, with a share of 68.2%, increased by 18.4%,
compared to 22.5% in 2021. It should be noted that the growth rate
of the average annual balance of demand against the economy in
foreign currency decreased compared to 2021, but recalculated in
local currency increased.
Evolution of new loans granted by the licensed banks
Annual dynamics of new loans granted by licensed banks in 2022
reduced due to a decrease in lending in domestic currency (Chart
3.11). The tightening of lending in domestic currency was largely
inﬂuenced by the continued increase in interest rates (Chart 3.18)
and the tightening of lending standards.
This impact was
counterbalanced by the increase in the dynamics of foreign
currency loans linked to the exchange rate (sector with relatively
stable interest rates) (Chart 3.13). As a result, the dollarisation
degree of loans has increased.
In 2022, the total volume of new loans granted by licensed banks
in national currency had a downward trend and amounted to MDL
30,062.4 million, 7.7% lower than in 2021, amid the continuous
process of increases in interest rates and lending standards (Chart
3.11). Thus, the share of the component in MDL amounted to
63.1% compared to 72.4% in 2021.
35Demands on the national economy represent claims on the banking system of
the Republic of Moldova (including banks in the process of liquidation), in national
and foreign currency, on non-ﬁnancial corporations with majority public or private
capital, on the non-bank ﬁnancial sector and on other resident sectors. This chapter
includes loans and borrowings (including outstanding debts), interest arrears on
loans; debt securities and shares; other claims.


Chapter 3. Monetary policy
51
Chart 3.13:

and linked to the exchange rate

An even more pronounced decline was recorded in loans to
individuals (Chart 3.12). The contraction of lending in this sector
has led to a reduction in the share of loans in MDL to individuals to
35.3% in 2022 from 48.7% in 2021.
At the same time, the
component of loans to legal entities recorded an annual growth
rate of 16.5% in 2022 (Chart 3.12).
In the reporting year, the volume of loans granted in foreign
currency and linked to the exchange rate, recalculated in MDL,
was 41.6% higher than in 2021. The total volume of loans granted
in foreign currency, including foreign exchange rate-linked loans,
recalculated in MDL, amounted to MDL 17,575.7 million (Chart
3.13). The dynamics were inﬂuenced by relatively stable interest
rates in this sector. In line with previous years, the majority share
of the volume of foreign currency loans, recalculated in MDL and
linked to the exchange rate, belongs to loans granted to legal
entities. In 2022, the share of these loans amounted to 96.8%,
lower than in 2021 by 1.4 percentage points.
Therewith, the
volume of loans granted to individuals, in foreign currency and
linked to the exchange rate, increased by 2.5 times in 2022.
Deposit market
The annual change in the average balance of deposits decreased in
2022 due to both structural currency components (Chart 3.14). The
average annual balance of deposits in MDL grew at an annual rate
of 3.2%, compared to 17.1% in 2021. It should be noted that at the
end of the reporting year, due to the acceleration in attracting new
deposits in the national currency, along with successive increases
in interest rates, the growth rate of the balance of term deposits
in MDL amounted to 23.8%.
From
the
perspective
of
the
term
of
the
placement,
the
development of the total average balance of deposits was
inﬂuenced by the gradual decrease in the annual growth rate of
the balance of sight deposits and the negative annual dynamics of
term deposit balances in the period March-October 2022. These
developments led to a decline in the annual growth rates of the
average annual balance of both term and sight deposits (Chart
3.13).
Evolution of new term deposits attracted by the licensed
banks
In 2022, new term deposits attracted by licensed banks showed
a pronounced upward dynamic compared to the previous year.
The growth rate of term deposits exceeded that of loans. The
driving factor behind this development was the rise in interest
rates, which led to a reduction in the volume of new loans granted
and encouraged saving.
The total volume of term attracted deposits amounted to MDL
46,298.3 million, 57.5% higher than in the previous year. As a
result of interest rate increases, in 2022, the volume of deposits in
the domestic currency segment increased by 66.6% and amounted
to MDL 31,816.7 million (Chart 3.16). Acceleration of attracted
deposits was in the second half of 2022 with more pronounced
increases in interest rates (Chart 3.18).


52
Annual Report (NBM, 2022)
In the segment of deposits attracted in national currency from
individuals, the annual growth was 85.6%.
The share of term
deposits attracted from individuals increased to 74.1% from 66.5%
in 2021. The volume of term deposits attracted from legal entities
increased by 28.8% compared to 2021.

Dynamics of volume and weighted

Source: NBM

Source: NBM
Chart 3.18: Average interest rates in MDL (% annualy)
Source: NBM
The segment of term deposits in foreign currency recalculated
in MDL also recorded increases (Chart 3.17). Compared to 2021,
the total volume of new deposits attracted in foreign currency
recalculated in MDL increased by 40.6%, due to the signiﬁcant
increase in deposits attracted from legal entities. The share of
term deposits attracted in foreign currency from legal entities
increased to 38.3%, compared to 15.0% in 2021.
Interest rates on new deposits and loans granted by the
licensed banks
The year 2022 was marked by increases in interest rates on the
market for term deposits and loans granted as a result of the
situation in the money market, including successive increases in
the base rate in order to moderate inﬂation.
In January-August 2022, the Executive Board of the National Bank
of Moldova gradually increased the base rate applied to the main
monetary policy operations in order to alleviate persistent
inﬂationary pressures, anchor inﬂationary expectations, stimulate
savings and reduce the depreciation of the national currency
following the widening of the current account deﬁcit. As a result,
from the level of 6.50% per annum, there followed three stages of
gradual increases of 2.0 percentage points in January-March 2022,
and then three stages of +3.0 percentage points in May-August
2022. Until November 2022, the base rate applied to the main
monetary policy operations was kept at 21.50% per annum. In the
last month of the year, the Executive Board of the National Bank
of Moldova lowered the base rate on the main monetary policy
operations by 1.5 percentage points to 20.00% per annum (Chart
3.18).
Interest rates on new loans granted by the licensed banks
The nominal weighted average interest rate on new loans granted
in MDL in 2022 was 11.73% per annum, 4.33 percentage points
higher than in 2021 (Chart 3.11).
The minimum value of the
weighted average nominal interest rate on new loans in MDL was
recorded in the ﬁrst month of the year (9.09% annually), and the
maximum rate – in October (14.31% annually) (Chart 3.18). For
2022, the weighted average interest rate on new loans in MDL
granted to legal entities was 11.28% per annum, higher than
in 2021 by 3.04 percentage points, and the weighted average
interest rate on loans in MDL granted to individuals – 12.54% per
annum, increasing by 6.01 percentage points compared to the
previous year.
For the whole of 2021, the weighted average rate on loans in
foreign currency and linked to the exchange rate was 4.31% per
annum, 0.27 percentage points higher than in 2021 (Chart 3.13).


Chapter 3. Monetary policy
53
Interest rates on new term deposits
More pronounced increases in interest rates in the domestic
currency segment compared to new loans in MDL were recorded
for term deposits. The development reﬂects the rise in interest
rates on both retail and corporate deposits.
In 2022, interest rates on new term deposits attracted in national
currency by licensed banks ranged from 4.86% per annum in
January to 13.61% per annum in November 2022 (Chart 3.18).

balances, at the end of the year (% annualy)
Source: NBM
Chart 3.20: Money velocity (percentage points)
Source: NBM
The weighted average rate of term deposits attracted in national
Chart 3.21: Money multiplier indicators (percentage
points)
Source: NBM
currency was 10.61% annually, 7.13 percentage points higher
than in 2021 (Chart 3.16). At the sectoral level, the weighted
average interest rate on deposits attracted in national currency
from individuals was 12.27% per annum, higher than in 2021 by
8.32 percentage points and ranged from 5.45% per annum
(January) to 15.62% per annum (December).
The weighted
average interest rate on deposits attracted in national currency
from legal entities was 5.86% per annum, 3.30 percentage points
higher than in 2021.
The weighted average interest rate on term deposits attracted in
foreign currency for 2022 was 1.11% per annum, 0.64 percentage
points higher than in the previous year (Chart 3.17).
Interest rates on balances
The effect of the successive increases in the NBM base rate since
2022 and the situation created on the money market was also
reﬂected in the increase of the weighted average interest rates on
the MDL balance of both term deposits and loans granted by the
banking system (Chart 3.19).
The weighted average interest rate on balance of loans in national
currency at the end of 2022 was 11.30% per annum, 3.97
percentage points higher than at the end of 2021. The weighted
average interest rate on balance of loans in foreign currency and
linked to the foreign exchange rate was 4.71% per annum, 0.49
percentage points higher than at the end of 2021.
A more pronounced increase in the national currency sector was
recorded in the weighted average interest rate on the balance of
term deposits in MDL. At the end of the year, this interest rate
stood at 11.06% annually, 7.32 percentage points higher than at
the end of the previous year.
The weighted average interest rate on the balance of foreign
currency deposits at the end of 2022 increased compared to the
end of 2021 and amounted to 1.05% per annum.
It should be noted that, as a result of all the developments on the
money market, for the whole of 2022, the impact of the increase in
interest rates on new transactions was also reﬂected in increases
in the weighted average rates on balances, so that at the end
of the year the weighted average interest rate on the balance of
loans in MDL was only 0.24 percentage points higher than the
weighted average interest rate on the balance of term deposits.


54
Annual Report (NBM, 2022)
Monetary indicators
In 2022, the money velocity indicators continued the acceleration
trend that began in the previous year. This acceleration trend
was more pronounced in the ﬁrst half of the year, driven by the
increase in real economic activity, as well as a higher growth
rate of nominal GDP compared to the growth rate of monetary
aggregates. Towards the end of the year, these trends became
less pronounced.
Chart 3.22: Monetization coefﬁcient of the economy
Source: NBM
Chart 3.23: Economy lending coefﬁcient and the
degree of indebtedness (%)
Source: NBM
Chart 3.24: Degree of dollarization in the economy
(%)
Source: NBM
The velocity of circulation of monetary aggregate M1 increased
by 0.31 percentage points to 4.3 points (Chart 3.20) and the
velocity coefﬁcient of monetary aggregate M2 increased by 0.08
percentage points to 3.0 percentage points. Therewith, the velocity
of M3 recorded 2.2 percentage points, 0.14 percentage points
higher than in the previous year.
As regards monetary multipliers, in the year under review they
reversed the trend recorded in recent years, due to the decrease
in demand for credit from the real sector of the national economy
and the demand for consumer credit from individuals, as well as
the increase in the monetary base. In 2022, the most pronounced
decrease was in the M3 money multiplier – by 0.36 percentage
point to 2.20 percentage points (Chart 3.21), the M2 money
multiplier decreased by 0.18 percentage points to 1.59 percentage
points and the M1 money multiplier reached 1.11 percentage
points, 0.19 percentage points below the level recorded in the
previous year.
After
an
accelerated
growth
during
2021,
the
economy’s
monetisation coefﬁcient in the ﬁrst three quarters of 2022 showed
a decelerating trend across all components, with a partial recovery
towards the end of 2022. This development was driven by the
stimulative/ tightening cycle of monetary policy during 2021-2022.
Thereby, at the end of 2022, the M3 monetisation coefﬁcient of
the economy reached 0.46 percentage points (Chart 3.22), 0.03
percentage points below last year’s level. The M2 monetisation
coefﬁcient was 0.33 percentage points, 0.01 percentage points
below the 2021 level. The M0 monetisation coefﬁcient was 0.13
percentage points, similar to the previous year.
The degree of indebtedness of the economy during 2022 decreased
compared to the previous year by 0.8 percentage points to 23.6%.
This development in the indebtedness of the economy was due
to the fact that the growth rate of loans granted by the banking
sector was below the growth rate of economic activity.
Therewith, the economy’s lending coefﬁcient (the ratio of loans
to deposits) increased during 2022 by 3.1 percentage points to
70.1% (Chart 3.23), due to higher rates of loan growth than deposit
growth.
The degree of dollarisation of the balance of loans granted in the
economy reversed its downward trend and at the end of 2022
accounted for 35.2% of total balance of loans, higher than at the
end of the previous year by 4.2 percentage points, as a result of a
more pronounced increase in foreign currency lending during 2022.
Dollarisation of deposits decreased by -3.4 percentage points to
38.2% at the end of the year (Chart 3.24).


Chapter 3. Monetary policy
55
3.5
Monetary market
Primary market of state securities
In 2022, it its capacity as the agent of the state for organizing
the placement of state securities in book-entry form, the National
Bank organized and conducted 129 auctions for the placement of
state securities.
Various factors, including improved investor perceptions following
the second-half 2021 election and a temporary decrease in risk
appetite
due to the outbreak of war in Ukraine, were partially
Chart 3.25: Quantitative indicators of the primary
market of SS (MDL million)
Source: NBM
Chart 3.26: Monthly dynamic of the primary market
indicators of SS
Source: NBM
Chart 3.27: Structure of SS issuance by maturity in
2021
Source: NBM
balanced by the increased attractiveness of these placements,
inﬂuenced by monetary policy decisions. As a result, the Ministry
of Finance decided to maintain the announced volume of state
securities in auctions throughout the year at a level quasi-similar
to that of December 2021.
In 2022, the ratio between the volume requested and the volume
announced at auctions became over unity as a result of the
signiﬁcant increase in investor interest in such placements,
fuelled by the surge in interest rates on them. However, in its
attempt to counter the increase in interest rates, the Ministry of
Finance rejected some of the bids submitted in the auctions and
placed a lower volume of SS than announced. Even under these
conditions, the total volume of securities put into circulation
increased, reaching a new historical high of MDL 26,288.1 million
(Chart 3.25).
Interest rates on the SS market followed a speciﬁc upward trend
for the end of 2021, boosted by increases in the NBM base rate,
reaching their highest value in September 2022. Future inﬂationary
expectations stated by the NBM in its monetary policy decisions
in August-September gave impetus to the start of a downward
trend in interest rates in this segment, which gained momentum
in December, following the start of the monetary policy easing
process (Chart 3.26).
The pronounced expression of the interest of non-bank investors
for placements in SS was also illustrated by the increase, to the
highest values of recent years, of the volumes purchased by them
during 2022 – MDL 6,687.0 million (MDL 1, 863.7 million in 2021)
or a quarter of the SS placed (7.4% in 2021).
Under the speciﬁc conditions of 2022, risk-averse investors
predominantly favoured SS with maturities of up to one year,
comprising the largest share in total SS issued. At the same time,
the sharp decline in investor interest for 5 and 7-year GB, evident
in the limited demand and oversupply from investors, prompted
the Ministry of Finance to cease their issuance starting in
September 2022. This decision led to a reduction in the average
maturity of issued securities, declining from 281 days to 253 days
(Charts 3.27 and 3.28).


56

The interest rates on the SS continued to rise, primarily inﬂuenced
by the relatively faster pace of the monetary policy interest rate
cycle, until the end of September. During this period, they mostly
oscillated near the upper limit of the corridor deﬁned by the
interest rates on the standing facilities offered by the NBM.
Subsequently, a period of quasi-stability ensued, which was
followed by a swift decline in rates after the NBM’s decision to
reintroduce monetary policy easing measures in early December
(Chart 3.29).
The annual average has also seen a notable increase: from 5.63%
in 2021 to 17.76% in 2022. The magnitude of the increases was
also similar from a perspective of securities’ maturity perspective,
with comparable sized adjustments for all maturities: 11.18; 11.96
and 12.43 percentage points for 91-, 182- and 364-day bonds,
respectively.
The annual weighted average nominal interest rates on SS traded
during the last two years at primary market auctions, split by
maturity, are shown in the table 3.1.
The development of interest rates on SS in 2022 has changed the
hierarchy of money market yields established in previous years.
Investments in state securities took the lead, offering higher yields
compared to new loans.
Consequently, from the beginning of
the year and throughout its entirety, interest rates on securities
outperformed those on new loans, maintaining a considerable gap
between them. Simultaneously, there was a notable narrowing of
the spread between interest rates on loans disbursed and interest
rates on deposits attracted, primarily due to the more substantial
increase in the latter (Chart 3.30).
The volume of state securities, placed on the primary market
through auctions, outstanding as of 31 December 2022, amounted
to MDL 20,189.8 million at nominal value (Chart 3.31). At the sale-
purchase price, it amounted to MDL 17,908.6 million, recording
a decrease of MDL 143.4 million at the end of the management
year compared to the end of the previous year. As of 31 December
2022, of the SS outstanding, those with a residual maturity of up
to three months constituted 28.9%, from 3 to 6 months – 31.3%,
from 6 to 12 months – 31.7%, over 1 year – 8.1%

58
Annual Report (NBM, 2022)
Secondary market for state securities
The total volume of secondary market transactions for the whole
of 2022 recorded a decrease of 64.3% compared to the previous
year, amounting to MDL 466.6 million (2021: MDL 1,308.0 million),
with the maximum monthly value (MDL 131.3 million) recorded in
January (Chart 3.32).
The yields on state securities with a maturity of up to one year
traded on the secondary market followed the trend of the primary
market of the SS, which in turn were responsive to the development
of the NBM monetary policy rates. Thus, the maximum level of
21.97% was reached in September, while in May the rate curve
was discontinuous, during which no transactions were recorded.
The average annual interest rate stood at 14.39% (2021: 5.55%),
as the weighted average term decreased to 192 days (2021: 205
days).
As regards the structure of the secondary market’s traded
securities by maturity, the most traded securities continue to be
those with maturities of up to one year (Charts 3.33 and 3.34). In
this context, compared to the previous year, the absence of the
2-years GB in the curve is observed, with a shift in investor
preferences towards half-year securities.
Evolution of interbank market indicators in Moldovan lei
Reference
interest
rates
applied
on
interbank
money
market
The benchmark rate curve CHIBOR/CHIBID (calculated based on
the indicative interest rates for placing/attracting funds in national
currency on the interbank money market) reacted promptly to
changes in the NBM monetary policy rate during 2022.
Under the restrictive decision making that started in July 2021,
CHIBOR 2W rates continued to increase in the period January-
September 2022, but at a faster pace, intensely reﬂecting the
increases in the base rate during this period.
In October and
November, it slows down, hovering in the upper part of the interest
rate corridor. In the second half of December, the CHIBOR yield
curve returned to a downward path under the impact of the decline
in the base rate, but its amplitude was modest. The CHIBOR 2W
rate at the end of the year was signiﬁcantly higher than on the last
day of the previous year (+14.04 percentage points), at 22.23%
(Chart 3.35).
Interbank loans/deposits market in MDL
During the reporting year, no transactions were recorded on the
interbank market for loans/deposits in national currency.

Foreign exchange market

Evolution of the foreign exchange market of the Republic
of Moldova
The total turnover of currencies traded against MDL maintained
the rapid pace of growth, increasing by 28.8% to the equivalent
of USD 23,091.1 million (Table A.6). This growth can be attributed
to the intensiﬁcation of economic transactions with the external
sector, including international trade in goods and services, foreign
credits and grants, investments, and remittances.
Turnover in giro transactions increased at a faster pace (+35.5%)
than turnover in cash transactions (+10.1%), thus contributing to
an increase in the share of foreign exchange turnover by transfer
in the total to 77.6% (Chart 3.36).
The structure of giro transactions by type of counterparty did
not essentially change compared to the previous year, with the
major share still held by legal entities, 96.3% of the turnover of
transactions, while 7.7% went to individuals, 0.7% more than in
2021.
As regards foreign exchange operations in cash, 62.2% were
carried out through foreign exchange ofﬁces of licensed banks.
Euro continued to be the preferred currency for foreign exchange
operations, holding the major share in the currency structure of
the total turnover of the domestic foreign exchange market –
54.5%, up by 1.2% compared to the previous year (Charts 3.37
and 3.38). In 2022, amid the events caused by the outbreak of the
war by Russia against Ukraine, the share of the Russian ruble in
market turnover fell below 1.0%. The euro continued to dominate,
holding a major share of 71.9% in the cash segment of the foreign
exchange market. Moreover, in 2022, it managed to surpass the
US dollar in the money order segment as well, accounting for
49.5% compared to 47.7% (Table A.7).
The European single currency also maintained its dominance in
terms of the number of transactions.
Around 70% of foreign
exchange transactions with non-bank legal entities were carried
out against the euro (Chart 3.39).
The majority of transactions, in terms of quantity, involve the euro
for amounts up to the equivalent of USD 500 thousand. However,
for larger amounts, transactions continue to predominantly use US
dollars (Charts 3.40 and 3.41).
The turnover of foreign exchange operations against MDL carried
out by licensed banks amounted to USD 21,138.3 million, up by
31.9% compared to the previous year.


60
Annual Report (NBM, 2022)
Chart 3.39: The total number of foreign exchange
transactions with legal entities, by currency
Source: NBM
Chart 3.40: The number of transactions in euro and
US dollars on the intra-bank foreign exchange market
by transfer, structured according to the amount traded

Source: NBM

by transfer, structured according to the amount traded

Source: NBM
The turnover of transactions involving legal entities, which account
for the major share of transactions carried out by licensed banks,
increased by 23.3%. Purchases from economic agents showed a
more signiﬁcant increase (+32.3%) compared to sales to them
(+18.5%). This reﬂects not only faster growth in exports compared
to imports, but also inﬂows of external grants and loans to address
the energy crisis and manage the inﬂux of refugees from Ukraine.
In addition, the turnover of transactions involving individuals and
foreign exchange ofﬁces increased in similar proportions, by 23.5%.
Notably, there was a higher increase in sales (+49.2%) compared
to purchases (+16.7%), a trend that became evident during the
period of turbulence on the foreign exchange market, particularly
in the ﬁrst quarter.
An upward dynamic has also emerged in the interbank segment
of the domestic foreign exchange market, both among resident
banks (+13.8%) and non-resident banks (+94.8%). Their share in
the total turnover of licensed banks amounted to 5.2%.
Overall, there was a shortage of foreign exchange on the domestic
foreign exchange market, with net purchases of foreign exchange
from individuals, foreign exchange ofﬁces and other sources (USD
2,750.8 million) falling below the level of net demand for foreign
exchange from economic agents (USD 3,010.2 million36). In these
circumstances, during the year, the NBM intervened through net
sales of foreign exchange to licensed banks in the amount of USD
249.8 million37. The NBM intervened through foreign exchange
sales mainly in the ﬁrst quarter, when the negative gap between
supply and demand was at its widest, and the NBM’s foreign
exchange purchases occurred mostly in the third quarter, when
supply exceeded demand, driven by seasonal factors and the
phenomenon of dedollarization (Table A.8).
Dynamics of foreign exchange assets and liabilities of
licensed banks
During
the
reporting
year,
the
balance-sheet
foreign
exchange assets and liabilities38 of the licensed banks
decreased, so that as of 31 December 2022, their balances
amounted to USD 2,359.4 million and USD 2,327.6 million,
respectively, down 1.3% and 2.7% from 31 December 2021,
respectively. Excluding exchange rate differences39, the opposite
dynamic was recorded, with balance sheet foreign exchange
assets and liabilities increasing by 3.4% and 1.9%, respectively.
As a share of banks’ total assets and liabilities, both indicators
decreased compared to the end of 2021. Thus, balance-sheet
foreign currency assets amounted to 34.4% of total assets (-1.4
percentage points), and balance sheet foreign currency liabilities –
to 41.2% (-1.6 percentage points) of total liabilities.

Balance sheet foreign currency assets and liabilities remain
predominantly denominated in euro. In 2022, the share of the
European single currency has slightly decreased to over 70.0% in
the structure of both indicators (Charts 3.42 and 3.43).
In the structure of assets by items, the downward trends in banks’
foreign currency holdings were observed, largely on the back
of the NBM’s decision to increase the foreign currency reserve
requirement ratio by 15.0 percentage points to discourage foreign
currency intermediation. Thus, at the end of the reporting year,
liquid assets amounted to USD 396.0 million, down 54.3% from
the previous year, mainly those held in "Nostro" and overnight
accounts in foreign banks (Tables A.9 and A.10). At the end of the
reporting year, foreign currency assets accounted for 16.8% of
total balance sheet foreign currency assets.
With regard to the currency structure of local banks’ liquid assets,
as of 31 December 2022, the European single currency accounted
for the largest share – 53.3%, followed by the US dollar – 42.5%,
the Russian ruble – 0.4% and other currencies – 3.8%.
The increase in the foreign currency required reserve ratio by 15.0
percentage points, to 45.0% of funds attracted in freely convertible
foreign currency subject to reservation, resulted in an increase
in the foreign currency required reserve balance to USD 917.2
million as of 31 December 2022, 51.7% more than at the end of
2021, constituting 38.9% of total balance sheet foreign currency
assets.
During 2022, overall, foreign currency lending activity by licensed
banks increased, but evolved differently by currency of lending.
Thus, the portfolio of loans granted in euro (including those granted
in MDL linked to exchange rate of MDL against euro) increased
by 22.9%40, while the portfolio of loans granted in US dollars
(including those granted in MDL linked to exchange rate of MDL
against the US dollar) contracted by 4.9%.
Under these conditions, the balance of foreign currency granted
loans increased by 16.5%41 to USD 1,082.8 million (of which USD
131.5 million were linked to the exchange rate). The balance of
foreign currency loans outstanding at the end of the reporting year
stood at 45.9%, about 7.0 percentage points higher than at the
last day of 2021.
On the liabilities side, there was an increase in funds attracted
by banks in US dollars (+10.9%), while those in euros decreased
insigniﬁcantly by 0.5% (excluding exchange rate differences).
40Excluding exchange rate differences, it increased by 31.3% at constant
exchange rate.
41Excluding exchange rate differences,
foreign currency lending activity
increased by 21.9%.

In the structure of the balance sheet foreign exchange liabilities of
licensed banks, 83.2% were attributed to traditional accounts,
including
term
and
sight
deposits
of
non-bank
customers
(hereinafter – foreign exchange accounts). As of the end of 2022,
the balance of foreign currency accounts stood at USD 1,936.5
million, which marked a decrease of 10.2%, and when excluding
exchange rate differences, a decrease of 5.9% compared to the
end of 2021 (Chart 3.45, tables A.11 and A.12). This decline was
primarily a result of individuals withdrawing funds from these
accounts during the local currency market turmoil, which was a
reaction to the Russian war against Ukraine.
In 2022, corporate bank customers preferred to reduce their
investments in sight accounts in favour of term deposits. The
balance of funds in term deposits of individuals decreased more
than in sight accounts, the decrease in the latter being tempered
by the increase in funds placed, mostly in US dollars, by non-
resident individuals (excluding exchange rate differences +88.0%
or about USD 26.0 million).
Respectively, the balance of funds held at sight in customer
accounts decreased by 9.9% (by 5.6% excluding exchange rate
differences) to USD 1,329.5 million, and the balance of term
deposits – by 10.7% (by 6.7% excluding exchange rate differences)
to USD 607.0 million.
Foreign currency term deposits attracted from banks’ non-bank
customers, mostly from individuals, covered 63.8% of foreign
currency loans granted by licensed banks (excluding those linked
to foreign exchange rates) compared to 83.2% at the end of 2021.
As of 31 December 2022, compared to 31 December 2021, banks’
foreign currency liabilities to individuals decreased by 6.5
percentage points as a share of total on-balance sheet foreign
currency liabilities, the balance of which decreased by 14.1%
(-9.9% excluding exchange rate differences) to USD 1,146.4
million.
In this regard, at the end of the reporting year, the
balance of foreign currency accounts of individuals and non-bank
legal entities stood at 49.3% and 33.9%, respectively, of the total
balance sheet foreign currency liabilities of licensed banks.
In 2022, the level of foreign currency credit attracted by banks
increased, signiﬁcantly, primarily from foreign sources, as credit
drawings by licensed banks exceeded repayments. Respectively,
the balance of loans received by banks increased by 63.4%
(excluding exchange rate differences) to USD 272.7 million.
At the end of the reporting year, 13.5% of licensed banks’ balance
sheet foreign currency liabilities were to non-residents compared
to 7.3% as of 31 December 2021.
Foreign currency assets and contingent liabilities at the end
of 2022 recorded insigniﬁcant amounts of USD 12.2 million and
USD 28.2 million, respectively, reﬂecting local bank commitments
to both currency/currency and currency/MDL conversions (Table
A.13).
